Figure 4
.
An illustration of the shift-invariance: the joint distributions of the three passage times/partition functions in the left and right panels are the same, under the condition that both endpoints of each path are shifted by the same amount, and the endpoints and shifts are such that the paths are all forced by planarity to intersect both before and after the shift.

The following remarkable shift-invariance property (illustrated in
Figure

4
) proved in
[
BGW22
]
will also be a key input. It follows from
[
BGW22
, Theorems 7.8, 7.10]
immediately.

Lemma 3.3
.

Take any
m\in\mathbb{N}
, and
x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{m}
,
y_{1}\geq\cdots\geq y_{m}
, and
x_{1}^{\prime}\leq\cdots\leq x_{m}^{\prime}
,
y_{1}^{\prime}\geq\cdots\geq y_{m}^{\prime}
, such that
x_{i}-y_{i}=x_{i}^{\prime}-y_{i}^{\prime}
for any
i\in[\![{1,m}]\!]
. Then
\{\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(x_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{m}
and
\{\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(x_{i}^{\prime},y_{i}^{\prime})\}_{i=1}^{m}
are equal in distribution.

We next give the behavior of
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}
under upper tail events. To be concise, for the rest of this section we fix
t>0
. All the constants (including all
C,c>0
) are allowed to depend on
t
.
And
\beta
is taken to equal either
1
or
\infty
.

3.1.
Coalescence and independent tents with Brownian bridges

We now give our main coalescence estimate, in the form of stating that the quadrangle inequalities from (
2.1
) and (
2.4
) are sharp under the upper tail. Its proof will be given in
Section

3.2
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Take any
L>0
and
L^{+}>L+\exp(-0.001L^{3/2})
(including
L^{+}=\infty
), and denote
H=10^{-6}L^{1/2}
.
When
\beta=\infty
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(x,y)=\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(x,0)+\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,y)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0),\;\forall|x|,|y|\leq H\ \Big|\ L<\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)<L^{+}\right)>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2}).

When
\beta=1
, the same bound holds when the event is replaced by

|\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(x,y)-(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(x,0)+\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,y)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0))|<C\exp(-cL),\quad|x|,|y|\leq H.

The lower bound on
L^{+}
above is to ensure a lower bound on
\mathbb{P}(L<\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)<L^{+})
which will be needed in the proof.

The connection between the above estimate and coalescence is that, at zero temperature (
\beta=\infty
), the equality is equivalent to the coalescence of a family of geodesics, according to
Lemma

2.4
.
At positive temperature, almost surely the quadrangle inequality is strict (see (
2.4
)), so the equality is replaced by an upper bound of
C\exp(-cL)
, which is roughly the probability for the corresponding polymers to be disjoint given the field
\mathcal{Z}
.

As we have seen from e.g.
Theorem

2.17
, there are tent behaviors under the upper tail event.
The following proposition states that conditional on the upper tail event, the two tents seen from both positive and negative directions are roughly independent, and are close to Brownian bridges.
It is a two-sided version of
Lemma

2.19
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Take any
L>0
and
L^{+}>L+\exp(-0.001L^{3/2})
(including
L^{+}=\infty
).
Let
H=10^{-6}L^{1/2}
.
Consider the following processes, each defined on
[0,H]
,

x\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,x)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0),\quad x\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-x)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0),

x\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(x,0)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0),\quad x\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-x,0)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0),

conditional on
L<\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)<L^{+}
. Further, also consider the processes (each defined on
[0,H]
as well)

x\mapsto B_{1}(x)+x(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0))/H,\quad x\mapsto B_{2}(x)+x(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0))/H,

x\mapsto B_{3}(x)+x(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(H,0)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0))/H,\quad x\mapsto B_{4}(x)+x(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-H,0)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0))/H,

also conditional on
L<\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)<L^{+}
, where
B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4}
are four rate
2
Brownian bridges in
[0,H]
, independent of each other and independent of
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}
.

There is another measure on
\mathcal{C}([0,H],\mathbb{R})^{4}
, such that, with probability
>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
, (1) its Radon-Nikodym derivative over the first set of processes is
1+O(\exp(-cL))
and (2) it can be coupled with the second set of processes such that, the
L^{\infty}
distance between them is
<C\exp(-cL)
.

The proof of
Proposition

3.5
involves reducing it to the following statement, using the shift-invariance of
Lemma

3.3
and
Proposition

3.4
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Take any
L>0
and denote
H=10^{-6}L^{1/2}
.
Consider the following processes, each defined on
[0,H]
,

x\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,x)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0),\quad x\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-x)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0),

x\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-x-H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-H),\quad x\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,x+H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,H),

conditional on
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)\in(L,L+\,\mathrm{d}L)
. Also, consider the processes (each defined on
[0,H]
as well)

x\mapsto B_{1}(x)+x(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0))/H,\quad x\mapsto B_{2}(x)+x(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0))/H,

x\mapsto B_{3}(x)+x(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-2H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-H))/H,\quad x\mapsto B_{4}(x)+x(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,2H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,H))/H,

also conditional on
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)\in(L,L+\,\mathrm{d}L)
, where
B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4}
are four rate
2
Brownian bridges in
[0,H]
, independent of each other and independent of
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}
.
They can be coupled so that under an event with probability
>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
for both, their Radon-Nikodym derivative is
1+O(\exp(-cL))
.

This lemma directly follows from
Lemma

2.19
, and we omit the details.
We now prove
Proposition

3.5
assuming
Proposition

3.4
and
Lemma

3.6
.

Figure 5
.
An illustration of transforming the weights of varying one side (left panel) into varying both sides (middle panel) using shift-invariance, then to two tents (right panel) by coalescence.

Proof of
Proposition

3.5
.

By shift-invariance (
Lemma

3.3
), in
Lemma

3.6
we can replace
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,x+H)
by
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-x,H)
and
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-x-H)
by
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(x,-H)
(see
Figure

5
).
Note that
Lemma

3.3
is stated in terms of finitely many points, but we can do the replacement for each
x\in[0,H]
simultaneously since
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}
is continuous.
Therefore we get the following statement.
Consider the processes

x\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,x)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0),\quad x\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-x)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0),

(3.2)

x\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(x,-H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-H),\quad x\mapsto\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-x,H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,H),

on
[0,H]
, conditional on
L<\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)<L^{+}
; and the processes (also on
[0,H]
)

x\mapsto B_{1}(x)+x(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0))/H,\quad x\mapsto B_{2}(x)+x(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0))/H,

(3.3)

x\mapsto B_{3}(x)+x(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(H,-H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-H))/H,\quad x\mapsto B_{4}(x)+x(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-H,H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,H))/H,

conditional on
L<\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)<L^{+}
, where
B_{1},B_{2},B_{3},B_{4}
are four rate
2
Brownian bridges in
[0,H]
, independent of each other and independent of
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}
.
They can be coupled so that under an event with probability
>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
for both, their Radon-Nikodym derivative is
1+O(\exp(-cL))
.

By
Proposition

3.4
, with probability
>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
, we have

|(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(x,-H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-H))-(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(x,0)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0))|<C\exp(-cL),

|(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-x,H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,H))-(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-x,0)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0))|<C\exp(-cL),

for any
x\in[0,H]
; in particular,

|(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(H,-H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-H))-(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(H,0)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0))|<C\exp(-cL),

|(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-H,H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,H))-(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-H,0)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0))|<C\exp(-cL).

By plugging these estimates into (
3.2
) and (
3.3
) respectively, we get the conclusion.
∎

It remains to prove
Proposition

3.4
, which we accomplish in the next subsection.

3.2.
Coalescence of polymers

Using the quadrangle inequalities (
2.1
) and (
2.4
),
Proposition

3.4
can be reduced to the following lemma.

Lemma 3.7
.

Take any
L>0
and
L^{+}>L+\exp(-0.001L^{3/2})
, and denote
H=10^{-6}L^{1/2}
.
Then when
\beta=\infty
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-H,-H)+\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(H,H)>\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-H,H)+\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(H,-H)\mid L<\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)<L^{+}\right)<C\exp(-cL^{3/2}).

And when
\beta=1
, the same estimate holds with the event replaced by

\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-H,-H)+\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(H,H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-H,H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(H,-H)>C\exp(-cL).

In the
\beta=\infty
setting, in light of
Lemma

2.4
, the event whose probability we wish to bound is equivalent to that (in the directed landscape) the geodesic from
(-t^{2/3}H,0)
to
(-t^{2/3}H,t)
and the geodesic from
(t^{2/3}H,0)
to
(t^{2/3}H,t)
are disjoint.
Such disjointness is unlikely to happen under the upper large since then geodesics tend to merge into the geodesic from
(0,0)
to
(0,t)
, shortly away from the endpoints.
When
\beta=1
, the event is instead interpreted as that the multi-point partition function from the spatial coordinates
-t^{2/3}H
and
t^{2/3}H
at time
0
to
-t^{2/3}H
and
t^{2/3}H
at time
t
is comparable to the product of the two individual ones.
This can be then understood as a positive temperature form of disjointness.

For multi-point passage times and multi-point partition functions of size
2
, we can only estimate them when each side of the endpoints is at a single point, using
Lemma

2.14
.
In the proof of
Lemma

3.7
we will need to upper bound those from
-t^{2/3}H,t^{2/3}H
at time
0
to
-t^{2/3}H,t^{2/3}H
at time
t
.
Our strategy is to do surgeries around time
0
and time
1
(see
Figure

6
): we instead upper bound those from
0,0
at time
-\varepsilon t
to
0,0
at time
(1+\varepsilon)t
. Here
\varepsilon>0
is a small constant.
Then we use the composition laws and lower bound various passage times and partition functions between time
-\varepsilon t
and
0
, and time
t
and
(1+\varepsilon)t
.
We note that such surgery arguments have appeared in the directed landscape and LPP models, see e.g.
[
Ham20
]
.

Figure 6
.
An illustration of the surgery, bringing the endpoints of multi-point passage times and multi-point partition functions together.

We now give the details.
For concreteness, we take
\varepsilon=10^{-6}
in the rest of this section.
First, we note that by taking
C
large and
c
small, it suffices to prove
Lemma

3.7
for large enough
L
.
By
Lemma

3.3
,
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-H,H)+\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(H,-H)
and
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)
have the same joint distribution as
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,2H)+\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,-2H)
and
\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)
.
Then by
Theorem

2.17
we have

(3.4)

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-H,H)+\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(H,-H)<(2-0.001)L-8L^{1/2}H\mid L<\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)<L^{+}\right)<\exp(-cL^{3/2}).

Also by
Theorem

2.13
, for large
L
we have

(3.5)

\mathbb{P}\left(L<\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)<L^{+}\right)>c\exp(-(4/3+0.005)L^{3/2}).

3.2.1.
Directed landscape (
\beta=\infty
) setting

In this case, we can assume
t=1
since the law of
\mathcal{S}^{\beta=\infty}_{t}
is independent of
t
. Recall that
\mathcal{S}^{\beta=\infty}_{1}=\mathcal{L}(\cdot,0;\cdot,1)
.
On the event in
Lemma

3.7
whose probability we wish to bound,

Lemma

2.2
and
Lemma

2.4
imply that

\mathcal{L}(-H,0;H,1)+\mathcal{L}(H,0;-H,1)<\mathcal{L}(-H,0;-H,1)+\mathcal{L}(H,0;H,1)=\mathcal{L}((-H,H),0;(-H,H),1).

Therefore, since we have lower bounded the LHS in (
3.4
), to prove
Lemma

3.7
it suffices to prove the following estimate.

Lemma 3.8
.

For
L>0
large enough and
H=10^{-6}L^{1/2}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\big[\mathcal{L}((-H,H),0;(-H,H),1)>1.99L\big]\\
<C\exp\big(-(4/3+0.01)L^{3/2}\big).

Using this lemma and (
3.5
), we can bound the probability of the same event conditional on
L<\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)<L^{+}
, by
C\exp(-0.005L^{3/2})
.
Then by (
3.4
), and noting that
(2-0.001)L-8L^{1/2}H>1.99L
, we get
Lemma

3.7
.

Proof of
Lemma

3.8
.

By using (
2.2
) twice (first with
y_{1}=-H
,
y_{2}=0
,
y_{3}=H
and then with
y_{1}=y_{2}=0
and
y_{3}=-H
), we have

\mathcal{L}((0,0),-\varepsilon;(-H,H),0)\geq\mathcal{L}((0,0),-\varepsilon;(0,0),0)+\mathcal{L}(0,-\varepsilon;-H,0)+\mathcal{L}(0,-\varepsilon;H,0)-2\mathcal{L}(0,-\varepsilon;0,0).

Denote
w:=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}((0,0),-\varepsilon;(0,0),0)>0)
.
There exists
c_{*}>0
, such that

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(0,-\varepsilon;-H,0)+H^{2}/\varepsilon<-c_{*}\Bigr),\;\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(0,-\varepsilon;H,0)+H^{2}/\varepsilon<-c_{*}\Bigr),\;\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(0,-\varepsilon;0,0)>c_{*}\Bigr)<w/4.

We note that
w,c_{*}
are independent of
L
.
Therefore

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}((0,0),-\varepsilon;(-H,H),0)>-2H^{2}/\varepsilon-4c_{*}\Bigr)>w/4.

Similarly,

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}((-H,H),1;(0,0),1+\varepsilon)>-2H^{2}/\varepsilon-4c_{*}\Bigr)>w/4.

Note that

\mathcal{L}_{2}(0,-\varepsilon;0,1+\varepsilon)\\
\geq\mathcal{L}((0,0),-\varepsilon;(-H,H),0)+\mathcal{L}((-H,H),0;(-H,H),1)+\mathcal{L}((-H,H),1;(0,0),1+\varepsilon).

So we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}_{2}(0,-\varepsilon;0,1+\varepsilon)>1.99L-4H^{2}/\varepsilon-8c_{*}\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}((-H,H),0;(-H,H),1)>1.99L\Bigr)w^{2}/16.

Using
Lemma

2.14
and the fact that
\mathcal{L}_{2}(0,-\varepsilon;0,1+\varepsilon)\leq 2\mathcal{L}(0,-\varepsilon;0,1+\varepsilon)
almost surely, we can bound the left-hand side by
\exp\big(-(4/3+0.01)L^{3/2}\big)
. Thus the conclusion follows.
∎

3.2.2.
Positive temperature (
\beta=1
) setting

Recall from
Section

2.2.2
the notation
\mathcal{M}
for multi-point partition functions. We can then write

(3.6)

\exp\big(t^{1/3}(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-H,-H)+\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(H,H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-H,H)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(H,-H))\big)-1\\
=\frac{(2t^{2/3}H)^{2}\mathcal{M}((-t^{2/3}H,t^{2/3}H),0;(-t^{2/3}H,t^{2/3}H),t)}{\exp\big(t^{1/3}(\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-H,H)+\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(H,-H))-t/6\big)}.

Therefore, to prove
Lemma

3.7
, the main task is to prove the following estimate.

Lemma 3.9
.

For
L>0
large enough and
H=10^{-6}L^{1/2}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\mathcal{M}\bigl((-t^{2/3}H,t^{2/3}H),0;(-t^{2/3}H,t^{2/3}H),t)\bigr)>\exp\bigl(t^{1/3}\cdot 1.99L\bigr)\Bigr)<C\exp\big(-(4/3+0.01)L^{3/2}\big).

Using this lemma and (
3.5
), we can bound the probability of the same event conditional on
L<\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)<L^{+}
, by
C\exp(-0.005L^{3/2})
.
Then by (
3.4
), conditional on
L<\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(0,0)<L^{+}
, with probability
>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
the expression (
3.6
) is upper bounded by

\frac{(2t^{2/3}H)^{2}\exp\big(t^{1/3}\cdot 1.99L\big)}{\exp\big(t^{1/3}\cdot(2L-8L^{1/2}H-0.001L)-t/6\big)}<C\exp(-cL).

Thus we get
Lemma

3.7
.

The rest of this section is devoted to proving
Lemma

3.9
.
We let
\mathcal{E}
denote the event whose probability we wish to bound, in the statement of
Lemma

3.9
.
By
Lemma

2.14
, we have

\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{Z}(0,-\varepsilon t;0,(1+\varepsilon)t)>\exp\big(t^{1/3}\cdot 0.98L\big),\;\mathcal{Z}_{2}(0,-\varepsilon t;0,(1+\varepsilon)t)>\exp\big(t^{1/3}\cdot 1.96L\big)\Big)\\
<C\exp\big(-(8/3-0.1)L^{3/2}\big).

By the continuity of
\mathcal{M}
(
Lemma

2.8
), we have almost surely

2^{-1}(1+2\varepsilon)t\mathcal{M}((-\delta,\delta),-\varepsilon t;(-\delta,\delta),(1+\varepsilon)t)\to\mathcal{Z}_{2}(0,-\varepsilon t;0,(1+\varepsilon)t),

as
\delta\to 0
from the right.
Then there exists small enough
\delta_{L}>0
, such that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{+})<C\exp\big(-(8/3-0.1)L^{3/2}\big)
, with
\mathcal{E}^{+}
being the event where

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(0,-\varepsilon t;0,(1+\varepsilon)t)

\displaystyle>\exp\big(t^{1/3}\cdot 0.98L\big),

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\bigl((-\delta_{L},\delta_{L}),-\varepsilon t;(-\delta_{L},\delta_{L}),(1+\varepsilon)t\bigr)

\displaystyle>\exp\big(t^{1/3}\cdot 1.98L\big).

We will show that conditional on
\mathcal{E}
, with positive probability (independent of
L
),
\mathcal{E}^{+}
holds.
For this, we define the following events.

By
Lemma

2.8
, there exists a small number
w>0
, such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{M}((-x,x),-\varepsilon t;(-w,w),0)>w\right)>w

for any
|x|\leq w
.
We let
\mathcal{E}_{1}
be the event where

\mathcal{M}\bigl((-\delta_{L},\delta_{L}),-\varepsilon t;(-w,w),0\bigr)>w,\quad\mathcal{M}\bigl((-w,w),t;(-\delta_{L},\delta_{L},(1+\varepsilon)t\bigr)>w.

Then
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1})>w^{2}
.

We let
\mathcal{E}_{2}
be the event where for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,

\left|(\varepsilon t)^{-1/3}(\log\mathcal{Z}((\varepsilon t)^{2/3}x,-\varepsilon t;(\varepsilon t)^{2/3}y,0)+\varepsilon t/12)+(x-y)^{2}\right|<10^{-3}L+\log(|x-y|+2),

and

\left|(\varepsilon t)^{-1/3}(\log\mathcal{Z}((\varepsilon t)^{2/3}x,t;(\varepsilon t)^{2/3}y,(1+\varepsilon)t)+\varepsilon t/12)+(x-y)^{2}\right|<10^{-3}L+\log(|x-y|+2).

Then by
Lemma

3.2
, we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2})>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
.

By
Lemma

3.1
and the shift invariance of
\mathcal{Z}
, we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{*})>1-C\exp(-cL^{2})
, for
\mathcal{E}^{*}
denoting the event where

\sup_{x,y\in[-H-1,-H]}\bigl|\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(x,y)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(-H,-H)\bigr|,\;\sup_{x,y\in[H,H+1]}\bigl|\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(x,y)-\mathcal{S}^{\beta}_{t}(H,H)\bigr|<10^{-10}L.

Lemma 3.10
.

We have
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}\cap\mathcal{E}^{*}\cap\mathcal{E}\subset\mathcal{E}^{+}
.

Proof.

In this proof we always take

x,x^{\prime}\in[-t^{2/3}(H+1),-t^{2/3}H],\quad y,y^{\prime}\in[t^{2/3}H,t^{2/3}(H+1)].

We will use
\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}
to lower bound
\mathcal{M}((-\delta_{L},\delta_{L}),-\varepsilon t;(x,y),0)
and
\mathcal{M}((x^{\prime},y^{\prime}),t;(-\delta_{L},\delta_{L}),(1+\varepsilon)t)
,
and
\mathcal{Z}(0,-\varepsilon t;x,0)
,
\mathcal{Z}(x^{\prime},t;0,(1+\varepsilon)t)
; then we will lower bound
\mathcal{M}((x,y),0;(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}),t)
and
\mathcal{Z}(x,0;x^{\prime},t)
for these
x,x^{\prime},y,y^{\prime}
, using
\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{E}^{*}
.
Then using the composition laws (
1.3
) and (
2.5
), we obtain
\mathcal{E}^{+}
.

Step 1.

Using
Lemma

2.7
, we have that

(y-x)\mathcal{M}((-\delta_{L},\delta_{L}),-\varepsilon t;(x,y),0)>2w\mathcal{M}((-\delta_{L},\delta_{L}),-\varepsilon t;(-w,w),0)\\
\times\frac{\mathcal{Z}(-\delta_{L},-\varepsilon t;x,0)\mathcal{Z}(\delta_{L},-\varepsilon t;y,0)}{\mathcal{Z}(-\delta_{L},-\varepsilon t;-w,0)\mathcal{Z}(\delta_{L},-\varepsilon t;w,0)}.

Under
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, the first factor in the right-hand side is
>2w^{2}
; and under
\mathcal{E}_{2}
, the second factor in the right hand side is
>\exp(-t^{1/3}10^{-3}L)
, when
L
is large enough.
Therefore, we have

(3.7)

\mathcal{M}((-\delta_{L},\delta_{L}),-\varepsilon t;(x,y),0)>c\exp(-t^{1/3}10^{-3}L).

By symmetry, the same lower bound holds for
\mathcal{M}((x^{\prime},y^{\prime}),t;(-\delta_{L},\delta_{L}),(1+\varepsilon)t)
.

The event
\mathcal{E}_{2}
also implies that

(3.8)

\mathcal{Z}(0,-\varepsilon t;x,0),\mathcal{Z}(x^{\prime},2t;0,(2+\varepsilon)t)>\exp(-t^{1/3}10^{-3}L).

Step 2.

Using
Lemma

2.7
, we have that

2\mathcal{M}((x,y),0;(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}),t)>\mathcal{M}((-t^{2/3}H,t^{2/3}H),0;(-t^{2/3}H,t^{2/3}H),t)\\
\times\frac{\mathcal{Z}(x^{\prime},0;x,t)\mathcal{Z}(y^{\prime},-0;y,t)}{\mathcal{Z}(-t^{2/3}H,0;-t^{2/3}H,t)\mathcal{Z}(t^{2/3}H,-0;t^{2/3}H,t)}.

By
\mathcal{E}
, the first factor in the right-hand side is
>\exp(t^{1/3}\cdot 1.99L)
; and by
\mathcal{E}^{*}
, the second factor in the right-hand side is

>\exp(-t^{1/3}10^{-9}L)
.
Therefore we have

(3.9)

\mathcal{M}((x,y),0;(x^{\prime},y^{\prime}),t)>c\exp(t^{1/3}\cdot(1.99-10^{-9})L).

From
\mathcal{E}
, we also have that

\mathcal{Z}(-t^{2/3}H,0;-t^{2/3}H,t)\vee\mathcal{Z}(t^{2/3}H,0;t^{2/3}H,t)>\exp(t^{1/3}\cdot 0.99L).

Without loss of generality, we assume that

\mathcal{Z}(-t^{2/3}H,0;-t^{2/3}H,t)>\exp(t^{1/3}\cdot 0.99L).

Then by
\mathcal{E}^{*}
, we have

(3.10)

\mathcal{Z}(x,0;x^{\prime},t)>\exp(t^{1/3}\cdot(0.99-10^{-9})L).

Step 3.

By the composition law (
2.5
), and (
3.7
), (
3.9
), we have

\mathcal{M}((-\delta_{L},\delta_{L}),-\varepsilon t;(-\delta_{L},\delta_{L}),(1+\varepsilon)t)>c\exp(t^{1/3}\cdot(1.99-2\cdot 10^{-3}-10^{-9})L).

By the composition law (
1.3
), and (
3.8
), (
3.10
), we have

\mathcal{Z}(0,-\varepsilon t;0,(1+\varepsilon)t)>c\exp(t^{1/3}\cdot(0.99-2\cdot 10^{-3}-10^{-9})L).

These imply the event
\mathcal{E}^{+}
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

3.9
.

By
Lemma

3.10
, and the fact that
\mathcal{E}_{1},\mathcal{E}_{2}
are independent of
\mathcal{E}^{*},\mathcal{E}
, we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{*}\cap\mathcal{E})\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{+})
.
Then by the bounds on
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1})
,
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2})
,
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{*})
,
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})
, and
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{+})
, we have that

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\leq C\exp\big(-(8/3-0.1)L^{3/2}\big)(w^{2}-C\exp(-cL^{3/2}))^{-1}-C\exp(-cL^{2}),

and this is bounded by
C\exp\big(-(4/3+0.01)L^{3/2}\big)
for
L
large enough.
∎

4.
Tail comparison estimates

To reduce notations, in the rest of this paper we denote

\mathcal{L}^{\beta=1}(x,s;y,t)=\log\mathcal{Z}(x,s;y,t)+(t-s)/12,\quad\mathcal{L}^{\beta=\infty}(x,s;y,t)=\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t).

We take
\beta=1
or
\infty
systematically unless otherwise noted.
From the above definition we have
\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,1}(x)=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x,t)
.

In this section,
t
is taken to be any
t>t_{0}
, where
t_{0}
is an arbitrary positive number. All the constants may depend on
t_{0}
, but are uniform in
t
.

As indicated in
Section

1.2
, the following tail ratio estimate would be central in much of our analysis.

Theorem 4.1
.

For any
L\geq 2
, and
0<\delta<L^{1/4}
,

(4.1)

\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L)}=\exp(-2\delta L^{1/2}+O(\delta L^{-1/4}\log(L)+L^{-3/2})).

For
\delta\geq L^{1/4}
, the same ratio equals
\exp(-\Omega(\delta L^{1/2}))
.

The main term
\exp(-2\delta L^{1/2})
comes from the following: from the tent behavior,
one considers a Brownian bridge in
[-L^{-1/2},L^{1/2}]
that equals
-L
at the two ends; then the ratio is roughly the probability that it is
>L+\delta
at
0
versus the probability that it is
>L
at
0
. As mentioned in the idea of proofs Section
1.2
, it can also be understood by Taylor expanding
\frac{4}{3}x^{3/2}
around
x=L
.

The general strategy will be to use the Gibbs property (
Lemma

2.9
) to resample
\smash{\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}}
in an interval
[-L^{1/2}+M,L^{1/2}-M]
, with some
M
large but much smaller than
L^{1/2}
.
The choice of
M
is such that,
conditioned on the upper tail large deviation event,
\smash{\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}}
in the interval
[-L^{1/2}+M,L^{1/2}-M]
is not much affected by the second line
\smash{\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,2}}
since, by the tent behavior, it will have obtained some separation from the second line by that time. Thus
\smash{\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}}
can be analyzed as a Brownian bridge on that interval.
For this, we need the following estimate of
\smash{\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}}
at the end point
-L^{1/2}+M
(and also for
L^{1/2}-M
by symmetry).

Lemma 4.2
.

For any
0<M<L^{1/2}
and
L\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(-L^{1/2}+M)\leq-(L^{1/2}-M)^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}M^{2}\ \Big|\ \hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right)<C\exp(-cM^{5/2}).

By the tent behavior, we expect the expectation of
\smash{\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(\pm(L^{1/2}-M))}
to be around
-L+2ML^{1/2}
, with a Gaussian fluctuation of order
M^{1/2}
.
This estimate bounds the probability that it is order
M^{2}
smaller than its mean.
The reason the upper bound is
\exp(-cM^{5/2})
rather than
\exp(-c(M^{2})^{2}/M)=\exp(-cM^{3})
is due to interactions with
\smash{\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,2}}
.

In the following proof we will use the notation
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}}[\cdot]:=\mathbb{E}[\,\cdot\mid\mathcal{F}]
and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}(\cdot):=\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid\mathcal{F})
for a
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}
. The existence of the regular conditional distribution is justified by the fact that the
\sigma
-algebras we consider will be generated by random variables taking values in Borel spaces and then invoking abstract existence results such as
[
Kal22
, Theorem 8.5]
.

Proof of
Lemma

4.2
.

Let
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}([-t^{2/3}L^{1/2},0])
(recall the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}
from
Section

2.3
) and
L_{M}=(L^{1/2}-M)^{2}
. Then we can write the probability of the complement of the event in the LHS in the statement of the lemma as

(4.2)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(-L_{M}^{1/2})>-L_{M}+\tfrac{1}{2}M^{2}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L}\right]}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L)}.

Let us focus on the conditional probability in the numerator. Let
\tilde{B}
be a Brownian bridge from
(-L^{1/2},\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(-L^{1/2}))
to
(0,\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0))
, such that
x\mapsto t^{1/3}\tilde{B}(t^{-2/3}x)
in
[-t^{2/3}L^{1/2},0]
interacts with
\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2}
by the Radon Nikodym derivative reweighting (
2.6
). Then the Brownian Gibbs property says that the conditional probability in the previous display equals

(4.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\tilde{B}(-L_{M}^{1/2})>-L_{M}+\tfrac{1}{2}M^{2}\right).

Next let
B
be a Brownian bridge from
(-L^{1/2},-L-M)
to
(0,L)
(with no lower boundary conditioning). Then, on the
\mathcal{F}
-measurable event

(4.4)

A(L,M)=\left\{\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(-L^{1/2})>-L-M,\;\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right\},

it holds that
\tilde{B}
dominates
B
by monotonicity (Lemma
2.10
), so (
4.3
) is lower bounded by

(4.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(B(-L_{M}^{1/2})>-L_{M}+\tfrac{1}{2}M^{2}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{A(L,M)}.

Now
B(-L_{M}^{1/2})
is distributed as a normal random variable with mean

-L-M+M\cdot\frac{L-(-L-M)}{L^{1/2}}=-L-M+2L^{1/2}M+M^{2}L^{-1/2}

and variance
\sigma^{2}=\frac{2M(L^{1/2}-M)}{L^{1/2}}\leq 2M
. Since
-L_{M}=-(L^{1/2}-M)^{2}=-L+2L^{1/2}M-M^{2}
, we see that (
4.5
) equals, on
A(L,M)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})>-M^{2}(\tfrac{1}{2}+L^{-1/2})+M\right)\geq 1-\exp\left(-cM^{3}\right)

using standard tail bounds for the normal distribution (Lemma
2.22
).
Putting this back in (
4.2
) and recalling the definition (
4.4
) of
A(L,M)
, we see that the LHS in the lemma is upper bounded by

\displaystyle 1-(1-\exp(-cM^{3}))\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(-L^{1/2})>-L-M\ \Big|\ \hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right).

By the FKG inequality (
Lemma

2.11
) and
Theorem

2.15
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(-L^{1/2})>-L-M\ \Big|\ \hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right)>1-\exp(-cM^{5/2})).

This completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

We can assume that
L
is large since otherwise the conclusion follows from Theorem
2.13
.
For some constant
C_{0}
sufficiently large, the case of
\delta>C_{0}L^{1/4}
also follows from Theorem
2.13
. In the remainder of the proof, we prove (
4.1
) for
\delta<C_{0}L^{1/4}
.

Denote
M=\log(L)
.
It suffices to show that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L)}\cdot\exp(2\delta L^{1/2})

\displaystyle<(1+CL^{-3/2})\exp(C\delta ML^{-1/4})\qquad\text{and}

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L)}\cdot\exp(2\delta L^{1/2})

\displaystyle>(1-CL^{-3/2})\exp(-C\delta ML^{-1/4}).

We let
L_{M}=(L^{1/2}-M)^{2}
, and
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}([-t^{2/3}L_{M}^{1/2},t^{2/3}L_{M}^{1/2}])
. We start by considering the ratio of conditional probabilities

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\right)}{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right)}.

We adopt the notation
\mathcal{B}
for the law of a Brownian bridge
B
from
(-t^{2/3}L_{M}^{1/2},\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,1}(-t^{2/3}L_{M}^{1/2}))
to
(t^{2/3}L_{M}^{1/2},\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,1}(t^{2/3}L_{M}^{1/2}))
, as well as the associated expectation.
With this notation, by the Brownian Gibbs property, the previous display equals

\frac{\mathcal{B}\bigl(\mathbbm{1}_{B(0)>t^{1/3}(L+\delta)}W(B,\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2})\bigr)}{\mathcal{B}\bigl(\mathbbm{1}_{B(0)>t^{1/3}L}W(B,\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2})\bigr)}=\frac{\mathcal{B}\bigl(B(0)>t^{1/3}(L+\delta)\bigr)}{\mathcal{B}\bigl(B(0)>t^{1/3}L\bigr)}\cdot\frac{\mathcal{B}\bigl(W(B,\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2})\mid B(0)>t^{1/3}(L+\delta)\bigr)}{\mathcal{B}\bigl(W(B,\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2})\mid B(0)>t^{1/3}L\bigr)},

where
W(B,\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2})
is the weight factor from (
2.6
).
Now the second ratio of terms in the previous display is lower bounded by
1
using stochastic monotonicity properties of Brownian bridges and that
W
is increasing in
B
. To upper bound the second ratio, we note that, since
W(B,\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2})\leq 1
,
it suffices to lower bound the denominator
\mathcal{B}\bigl(W(B,\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2})\mid B(0)>t^{1/3}L\bigr)
. To do this we consider the
\mathcal{F}
-measurable event
\mathrm{BdyCtrl}=\mathrm{BdyCtrl}(L,M)
defined by

\displaystyle\left\{\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(\pm L_{M}^{1/2})\geq-L_{M}+\tfrac{1}{2}M^{2}\right\}\cap\bigcap_{i=0}^{M^{-1}L_{M}^{1/2}}\left\{\sup_{|x|\in[L_{M}^{1/2}-iM,L_{M}^{1/2}-(i+1)M]}\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,2}(x)+x^{2}\leq(i+1)M\right\}.

By
Lemma

4.2
, the BK inequality (
Lemma

2.12
), and the upper bound on
\sup\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}
(following from
Theorem

2.13
and
Proposition

2.16
), we have

(4.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{BdyCtrl}^{c}\mid\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\right)\leq C\exp(-cM^{5/2})+\sum_{i=0}^{M^{-1}L_{M}^{1/2}}C\exp\left(-ci^{3/2}M^{3/2}\right)<C\exp(-cM^{3/2}).

Let
\ell^{-}
be the line joining
(-L_{M}^{1/2},-L_{M}+\frac{1}{4}M^{2})
and
(0,L-\frac{1}{4}M^{2})
,
\ell^{+}
be the line joining
(0,L-\frac{1}{4}M^{2})
and
(L_{M}^{1/2},-L_{M}+\frac{1}{4}M^{2})
, and
\ell
be their concatenation. Consider the high corridor event

\mathrm{HighCorr}=\left\{t^{-1/3}B(t^{2/3}x)\geq\ell(x)-L_{M}^{1/2}+|x|\text{ for all }x\in[-L_{M}^{1/2},L_{M}^{1/2}]\right\};

it is easy to obtain by standard Brownian bridge estimates that, on
\mathrm{BdyCtrl}
,
\mathcal{B}[\mathrm{HighCorr}\mid B(0)>t^{1/3}L]>1-C\exp(-cM^{2})
.

Now on
\mathrm{HighCorr}
and
\mathrm{BdyCtrl}
, we have
t^{-1/3}B(t^{2/3}x)\geq\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,2}(x)+\frac{1}{4}M^{2}-M
; and it is easy to check with the formula (
2.6
) that

W(B,\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2})>1-Ct^{2/3}L_{M}^{1/2}\exp(-ct^{1/3}M^{2})>1-C\exp(-cM^{2}).

Thus, these bounds on
W(B,\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2})
and
\mathcal{B}(\mathrm{HighCorr}\mid B(0)>t^{1/3}L)
yield that, on
\mathrm{BdyCtrl}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\bigl(W(B,\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2})\mid B(0)>t^{1/3}L\bigr)>1-C\exp(-cM^{2}).

So we see that, on
\mathrm{BdyCtrl}(L,M)
,

(4.7)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\right)}{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right)}=(1+O(e^{-cM^{2}}))\cdot\frac{\mathcal{B}\bigl(B(0)>t^{1/3}(L+\delta)\bigr)}{\mathcal{B}\bigl(B(0)>t^{1/3}L\bigr)}.

Let
\mu=\frac{1}{2}(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(-L_{M}^{1/2})+\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(L_{M}^{1/2}))
, and observe that
B(0)
under
\mathcal{B}
is distributed as a normal random variable with mean
t^{1/3}\mu
and variance
t^{2/3}L_{M}^{1/2}
.
Consider the
\mathcal{F}
-measurable event

\mathrm{MeanCtrl}(L)=\Bigl\{\mu\in[-L+2L^{1/2}M-ML^{1/4},-L+2L^{1/2}M+ML^{1/4}]\Bigr\}.

We know from Theorem
2.17
that

(4.8)

\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{MeanCtrl}(L)^{c}\mid\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta)<\exp(-cM^{2}).

Using standard bounds on the tail of the normal distribution (Lemma
2.22
), on
\mathrm{MeanCtrl}(L)
(and on
\mathrm{BdyCtrl}(L,M)
as we are assuming throughout), the RHS of (
4.7
) equals

\displaystyle(1+O(e^{-cM^{2}}))(1+O(L^{-3/2}))\cdot\frac{L-\mu}{L+\delta-\mu}\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2L_{M}^{1/2}}\Bigl[(L+\delta-\mu)^{2}-(L-\mu)^{2}\Bigr]\right)

and the last factor can be further written as

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\frac{2\delta(L-\mu)+\delta^{2}}{2L_{M}^{1/2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{2\delta(L+L-2L^{1/2}M+O(ML^{1/4}))+\delta^{2}}{2L_{M}^{1/2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-2\delta L^{1/2}+O(\delta ML^{-1/4})\right).

Overall, we have at this point established that, on
\mathrm{BdyCtrl}(L,M)\cap\mathrm{MeanCtrl}(L)
,

(4.9)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\right)}{\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right)}=(1+O(e^{-cM^{2}}))(1+O(L^{-3/2}))\cdot\frac{L-\mu}{L+\delta-\mu}\exp\left(-2\delta L^{1/2}+O(\delta ML^{-1/4})\right).

We next convert this into upper and lower bounds on
\mathbb{P}(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta)
, respectively.

Upper bound.

We see from (
4.9
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\right)\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{BdyCtrl}(L,M)\cap\mathrm{MeanCtrl}(L)}+\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathrm{BdyCtrl}(L,M)\cap\mathrm{MeanCtrl}(L))^{c}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq(1+Ce^{-cM^{2}})(1+CL^{-3/2})\exp\left(-2\delta L^{1/2}+C\delta ML^{-1/4}\right)\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\right)\mathbbm{1}_{(\mathrm{BdyCtrl}(L,M)\cap\mathrm{MeanCtrl}(L))^{c}},

so that, by taking expectations,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\bigl(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq(1+Ce^{-cM^{2}})(1+CL^{-3/2})\exp\left(-2\delta L^{1/2}+C\delta ML^{-1/4}\right)\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\left\{\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\right\}\cap\bigl(\mathrm{BdyCtrl}(L,M)\cap\mathrm{MeanCtrl}(L)\bigr)^{c}\Bigr).

We focus on the last term. It equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\Bigr)\cdot\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\bigl(\mathrm{BdyCtrl}(L,M)\cap\mathrm{MeanCtrl}(L)\bigr)^{c}\ \Big|\ \hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\Bigr).

If we can show that the second factor is small, we will be done. By a union bound, it is at most

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{BdyCtrl}(L,M)^{c}\mid\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta)+\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{MeanCtrl}(L)^{c}\mid\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta),

which, from (
4.6
) and (
4.8
), is upper bounded by
C\exp(-cM^{3/2})+\exp(-cM^{2})
.
This completes the proof of the upper bound on
\mathbb{P}(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta)
.

Lower bound.
We observe that, on
\mathrm{MeanCtrl}
,
\frac{L-\mu}{L+\delta-\mu}=\frac{2L+O(ML^{1/2})}{2L+O(ML^{1/2})+\delta}>1-C\delta L^{-1}
. Then we have, using (
4.9
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta)

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{BdyCtrl}(L,M)\cap\mathrm{MeanCtrl}(L)}

\displaystyle\geq(1-Ce^{-cM^{2}})(1-CL^{-3/2})\exp\left(-2\delta L^{1/2}-C\delta ML^{-1/4}\right)\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{F}}(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathrm{BdyCtrl}(L,M)\cap\mathrm{MeanCtrl}(L)},

absorbing the factor of
1-C\delta L^{-1}
into
\exp(-C\delta ML^{-1/4})
.
Taking expectations yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+\delta\right)

\displaystyle\geq(1-Ce^{-cM^{2}})(1-CL^{-3/2})\exp\left(-2\delta L^{1/2}-C\delta ML^{-1/4}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\mathbb{P}\bigl(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L,\mathrm{BdyCtrl}(L,M),\mathrm{MeanCtrl}(L)\bigr)

\displaystyle\geq(1-Ce^{-cM^{2}})(1-CL^{-3/2})\exp\left(-2\delta L^{1/2}-C\delta ML^{-1/4}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\mathbb{P}\bigl(\mathrm{BdyCtrl}(L,M),\mathrm{MeanCtrl}(L)\mid\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\bigr)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right).

As we saw above, the latter conditional probability is lower bounded by
1-C\exp(-cM^{3/2})
, thus completing the proof of the lower bound.
∎

5.
Tightness as continuous functions: geodesics and bounds for polymers

We next establish the following tightness of the relevant path measures.

As in
Theorems

1.1
and
1.2
, let
\pi_{0}
be the geodesic from
(0,0)
to
(0,1)
, in the directed landscape
\mathcal{L}^{\beta=\infty}
; and
\Gamma_{0}
be sampled from the annealed polymer measure from
(0,0)
to
(0,1)
, under
\mathcal{L}^{\beta=1}
.

Proposition 5.1

(Tightness)
.

As random elements in
\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
,
L^{1/4}\pi_{0}
or
L^{1/4}\Gamma_{0}
conditional on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L
for all
L\geq 2
are tight.

As mentioned in
Section

1.2
, to prove this requires tail bounds on two-point deviations, which rely on shear invariance of the directed landscape and the CDRP free energy field. The proof in zero temperature is much simpler than in positive temperature as in the former shear invariance alone suffices to give tightness on the
L^{-1/4}
scale. In positive temperature, the analogous argument only yields tightness on the
O(1)
scale, and additional arguments are needed to obtain the correct scale. The reason is that in zero temperature, given the environment, the path location at a given height is determined; while in positive temperature there is no a priori concentration of the polymer location.

In this section, we give the zero temperature proof
and some rough bounds for the transversal fluctuation of polymers.
The positive temperature part of
Proposition

5.1
will be proved in
Section

8
.

5.1.
Geodesic tightness

We start with a one-point estimate and later give the two-point estimate.

Lemma 5.2
.

For all
K>0
,
L\geq 2
, and
s\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\pi_{0}(s)|>K(s(1-s))^{1/2}L^{-1/4}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)<C\exp\left(-cK^{2}\right).

In fact, for
K
up to
L^{1/2}
we will obtain a tail of
\exp(-2K^{2})
(via the first case of the comparison statement Theorem
4.1
). Note that it exactly corresponds to our ultimate goal, namely that
L^{-1/4}\pi_{0}(s)
converges to
\frac{1}{2}N(0,s(1-s))
, which on scale
(s(1-s))^{1/2}
would have a tail at depth
x
that satisfies the approximate asymptotics of
\exp(-2x^{2})
.

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

Observe that, for any
K
,
\mathbb{P}(|\pi_{0}(s)|>K\mid\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L)
is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{|x|>K}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,s)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;0,1)\Bigr)>L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{x>K}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,s)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;0,1)\Bigr)>L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)

(5.1)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{x>K}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,s)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;0,1)\Bigr)>L\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L)},

the factor of 2 coming from a union bound and using the distributional symmetry of
\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,s)
and
\mathcal{L}(x,s;0,1)
under
x\mapsto-x
from Lemma
2.1
(as well as the independence of the two processes) to remove the absolute value in the condition under the supremum.
Then using the shear invariance and independence properties of
\mathcal{L}
and that
x>K
for the inequality in the second line,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,s)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;0,1)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(0,0;x-K,s)+\mathcal{L}(x-K,s;0,1)+(s(1-s))^{-1}\left[(x-K)^{2}-x^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{L}(0,0;x-K,s)+\mathcal{L}(x-K,s;0,1)-(s(1-s))^{-1}K^{2}

as a process in
x
. Thus we see that the RHS of (
5.1
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{x>0}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,s)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;0,1)\Bigr)>L+(s(1-s))^{-1}K^{2}\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L)}.

Now using that
\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,s)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;0,1)\Bigr)
from (
1.1
), it follows that the previous display is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L+(s(1-s))^{-1}K^{2}\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L)}.

Applying Theorem
4.1
now bounds the previous display by

C\exp\left(-cK^{2}(s(1-s))^{-1}L^{1/2}\right).

Replacing
K
by
K(s(1-s))^{1/2}L^{-1/4}
completes the proof.
∎

We next give a two-point estimate.
Combining it with the Kolmogorov-Chentsov criterion for tightness (see e.g.
[
Kal22
, Theorem 23.7]
), the
\beta=\infty
case of
Proposition

5.1
follows.

Proposition 5.3
.

For all
K>0
,
L\geq 2
, and
0<s<t<1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\pi_{0}(s)-\pi_{0}(t)|>K(t-s)^{1/2}L^{-1/4}\mid\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)\leq C\exp(-cK^{2}).

Proof.

We give the proof under the assumption that
t-s\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. The case where
t-s\in[\frac{1}{2},1]
follows from Lemma
5.2
easily.

Similar to the previous proof, the LHS of the display in the lemma is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{|x-y|>K}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,s)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)+\mathcal{L}(y,t;0,1)\Bigr)>L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{x-y>K}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,s)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)+\mathcal{L}(y,t;0,1)\Bigr)>L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)

(5.2)

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{x-y>K}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,s)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)+\mathcal{L}(y,t;0,1)\Bigr)>L\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)}.

Now, using the stationarity (and independence) properties of
\mathcal{L}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(0,0;x,s)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)+\mathcal{L}(y,t;0,1)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(-K,0;x-K,s)+\mathcal{L}(x-K,s;y,t)+\mathcal{L}(y,t;0,1)+(t-s)^{-1}\left[(x-y-K)^{2}-(x-y)^{2}\right]

as a process in
(x,y)
. Since
x-y>K
, we have that

(t-s)^{-1}\left[(x-y-K)^{2}-(x-y)^{2}\right]<-(t-s)^{-1}K^{2}.

Thus we see that the RHS of (
5.2
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle 2\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{x-y>K}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(-K,0;x-K,s)+\mathcal{L}(x-K,s;y,t)+\mathcal{L}(y,t;0,1)\Bigr)>L+(t-s)^{-1}K^{2}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)}

Now using that
\mathcal{L}(-K,0;0,1)=\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(-K,0;x,s)+\mathcal{L}(x,s;y,t)+\mathcal{L}(y,t;0,1)\Bigr)
, and that
\mathcal{L}(-K,0;0,1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)-K^{2}
, it follows that the previous display is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}(-K,0;0,1)>L+(t-s)^{-1}K^{2}\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L+((t-s)^{-1}+1)K^{2}\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)>L)}.

By Theorem
4.1
, this is upper bounded by

\displaystyle C\exp\left(-c(t-s)^{-1}K^{2}L^{1/2}\right).

Replacing
K
by
K(t-s)^{1/2}L^{-1/4}
completes the proof.
∎

5.2.
Polymer transversal estimates

We now adapt the zero temperature arguments above to the positive temperature setting.
Although the bounds in this subsection are not sufficient to derive the
\beta=1
case of
Proposition

5.1
, they will be used in the proof to be given in
Section

8
.

We start with the positive temperature analog of
Lemma

5.2
.

Lemma 5.4
.

For all
K>0
,
L\geq 2
and
s\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left[|\Gamma_{0}(s)|\geq K(s(1-s))^{1/2}\right]>\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}K^{2})\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)<C\exp(-cK^{2}L^{1/2}).

Remark 5.5
.

Unlike the zero-temperature case, here the concentration is on an
O(1)
scale. Some more work is needed to obtain an
L^{-1/4}
scale concentration, and we will turn to that shortly.

Proof of Lemma
5.4
.

By the convolution formula, for any
K>0
and
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left[|\Gamma_{0}(s)|\geq K\right]>\varepsilon\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{|x|\geq K}\mathcal{Z}(0,0;x,s)\mathcal{Z}(x,s;0,1)\,\mathrm{d}x>\varepsilon\mathcal{Z}(0,0;0,1)\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{|x|\geq K}\mathcal{Z}(0,0;x,s)\mathcal{Z}(x,s;0,1)\,\mathrm{d}x>\varepsilon e^{L-1/12}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)}

(5.3)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{x\geq K}\mathcal{Z}(0,0;x,s)\mathcal{Z}(x,s;0,1)\,\mathrm{d}x>\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon e^{L-1/12}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)},

where the last inequality uses the reflection symmetry of
\mathcal{Z}(0,0;\cdot,s)
and
\mathcal{Z}(\cdot,s;0,1)
and their independence. By the shear invariance and independence of the same two processes, we have that the following distributional equality holds as processes in
x
for every fixed
s
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(0,0;x,s)\mathcal{Z}(x,s;0,1)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{Z}(0,0;x-K,s)\mathcal{Z}(x-K,s;0,1)e^{(s(1-s))^{-1}[(x-K)^{2}-x^{2}]}

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{Z}(0,0;x-K,s)\mathcal{Z}(x-K,s;0,1)e^{-(s(1-s))^{-1}K^{2}},

where the inequality is due to that
x\geq K
. Substituting this into (
5.3
) yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left[|\Gamma_{0}(s)|\geq K\right]>\varepsilon\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{x\geq 0}\mathcal{Z}(0,0;x,s)\mathcal{Z}(x,s;0,1)\,\mathrm{d}x>\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon e^{L-1/12+(s(1-s))^{-1}K^{2}}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{Z}(0,0;x,s)\mathcal{Z}(x,s;0,1)\,\mathrm{d}x>\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon e^{L-1/12+(s(1-s))^{-1}K^{2}}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L+(s(1-s))^{-1}K^{2}+\log(\varepsilon/2)\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)}

We set
\varepsilon=\exp\left(-(s(1-s))^{-1}K^{2}/2\right)
and invoke Theorem
4.1
to obtain that the previous display is upper bounded by

\displaystyle C\exp\left(-cL^{1/2}(s(1-s))^{-1}K^{2}\right).

Replacing
K
by
K(s(1-s))^{1/2}
completes the proof.
∎

We next derive a two-point estimate.

Lemma 5.6
.

For all
K>0
,
L\geq 2
, and
0<s<t<1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left[|\Gamma_{0}(s)-\Gamma_{0}(t)|>K(t-s)^{1/2}\right]>\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2}K^{2})\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)<C\exp(-cK^{2}L^{1/2}).

Proof.

We give the proof under the assumption that
t-s\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, since case where
t-s\in[\frac{1}{2},1]
follows from
Lemma

5.4
easily. Observe that, for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left(|\Gamma_{0}(s)-\Gamma_{0}(t)|>K\right)\geq\varepsilon\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{|x-y|\geq K}\mathcal{Z}(0,0;x,s)\mathcal{Z}(x,s;y,t)\mathcal{Z}(y,t;0,1)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y>\varepsilon\mathcal{Z}(0,0;0,1)\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{|x-y|\geq K}\mathcal{Z}(0,0;x,s)\mathcal{Z}(x,s;y,t)\mathcal{Z}(y,t;0,1)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y>\varepsilon e^{L-1/12}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)}

(5.4)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{x-y\geq K}\mathcal{Z}(0,0;x,s)\mathcal{Z}(x,s;y,t)\mathcal{Z}(y,t;0,1)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y>\tfrac{1}{2}\varepsilon e^{L-1/12}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)},

where the factor of 2 comes from removing the absolute value in the condition under the supremum by a union bound and using that
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s;y,t)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(-x,s;-y,t)
,
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x,s)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;-x,s)
, and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(y,t;0,1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(-y,t;0,1)
each as processes in the relevant spatial variables, as well as the independence of all three processes on the LHS of the equalities.

Now, using the stationarity (and independence) properties of
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x,s)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s;y,t)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(y,t;0,1)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(-K,0;x-K,s)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x-K,s;y,t)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(y,t;0,1)

\displaystyle\qquad+(t-s)^{-1}\left[(x-y-K)^{2}-(x-y)^{2}\right]

as a process in
(x,y)
. Now since
x-y>K
, we see that

\displaystyle(t-s)^{-1}\left[(x-y-K)^{2}-(x-y)^{2}\right]<-(t-s)^{-1}K^{2}.

Thus the RHS of (
5.4
) is upper bounded by

(5.5)

\displaystyle 2\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{x-y>K}\mathcal{Z}(-K,0;x-K,s)\mathcal{Z}(x-K,s;y,t)\mathcal{Z}(y,t;0,1)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y>\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon e^{L-1/12+(t-s)^{-1}(K)^{2}}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)}.

Now using that

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(-K,0;0,1)-1/12=\log\int_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{Z}(-K,0;x-K,s)\mathcal{Z}(x-K,s;y,t)\mathcal{Z}(y,t;0,1)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}y,

and that
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(-K,0;0,1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)-K^{2}
, it follows that (
5.5
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle 2\cdot\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L+((t-s)^{-1}+1)K^{2}+\log(\varepsilon/2)\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L)}.

Set
\varepsilon=\exp(-(t-s)^{-1}K^{2}/2)
. Applying Theorem
4.1
gives that the previous display is upper bounded by

\displaystyle C\exp\left(-c(t-s)^{-1}K^{2}L^{1/2}\right).

Replacing
K
by
K(t-s)^{1/2}
completes the proof.
∎

6.
Proportionality and estimates on sums

In this section, we record a number of estimates on the sum of passage times or free energies.
Consider
(s_{1},\cdots,s_{k})\in\mathring{\Lambda}_{k}([0,\infty))
and
\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
For the convenience of notations we denote
s_{0}=y_{0}=0
, and adopt the shorthand
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,s_{k})
and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(s_{i-1},y_{i-1};s_{i},y_{i})
for each
1\leq i\leq k
.
Estimates in this section provide control on tail probabilities for the sum
\sum_{i}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}
(such as
Lemma

6.1
,
Lemma

6.4
) or on the deviation of each
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}
from
(s_{i}-s_{i-1})L
conditional on
\sum_{i}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}>L
(Lemma
6.3
).

We assume that
s_{k}<C_{0}
for a large
C_{0}
. All the constants (within this section) can depend on
k
and
C_{0}
.

Our first statement bounds the upper tail of
\sum_{i}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}
, in terms of the upper tail of
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

For any
L\geq 2
, any
M
, and any
\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, if
\min_{1\leq i\leq k}s_{i}-s_{i-1}>L^{-1}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{\vec{y}:\|\vec{y}-\vec{z}\|_{\infty}\leq L^{-2}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}>M,\;\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;z_{k},s_{k})<M-C\log L\right)<C\exp(-cL^{2}).

Proof.

This is immediate in the case of
\beta=\infty
by subadditivity, i.e.,
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;y_{k},s_{k})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}
and the unconditional local fluctuation bound (Lemma
3.1
) to obtain that
|\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;y_{k},s_{k})-\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;z_{k},s_{k})|\leq 1
with probability at least
1-\exp(-cL^{2})
.

We next turn to
\beta=1
.
We note from the unconditional fluctuation bound (
Lemma

3.1
) that, for any fixed intervals
I_{1},\ldots,I_{k}
, each with length
L^{-2}
, and each
z_{i}\in I_{i}
,

\inf_{\vec{y}\in\prod_{i=1}^{k}I_{i}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}>\sup_{\vec{y}:\|\vec{y}-\vec{z}\|_{\infty}\leq cL^{-2}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}-1,

with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cL^{2})
.
Under this event, and assuming that
\sup_{\vec{y}:\|\vec{y}-\vec{z}\|_{\infty}\leq L^{-2}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}>M
, we have (with
y_{k}=z_{k}
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;z_{k},s_{k})

\displaystyle\geq\log\int_{\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}I_{i}}\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\right)\,\prod_{y=1}^{k-1}\,\mathrm{d}y_{i}

\displaystyle>M-1+(k-1)\log(L^{-2}).

This completes the proof.
∎

By taking
L=M
and using
Theorem

2.13
, we get the following.

Corollary 6.2
.

For any large enough
L
, and any
\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, if
\min_{1\leq i\leq k}s_{i}-s_{i-1}>L^{-0.99}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{\vec{y}:\|\vec{y}-\vec{z}\|_{\infty}\leq L^{-2}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}>L\right)<\exp\left(-\frac{4}{3}s_{k}^{-1/2}L^{3/2}+Cs_{k}^{-1/4}L^{3/4}\right).

We note that the constraint
\min_{1\leq i\leq k}s_{i}-s_{i-1}>L^{-0.99}
is to ensure that the bound is much larger than
\exp(-cL^{2})
, the upper bound in
Lemma

6.1
.

The following statement asserts that, conditional on the sum of independent free energies being large, the individual terms are with high probability proportionate to the total, up to a certain scale of fluctuation.

Lemma 6.3
.

Fix each
y_{i}=0
.
For any
L
large enough,
K>C_{1}L^{3/8}
for a constant
C_{1}>0
(so that
KL^{1/4}>C_{1}L^{5/8}
), if
\min_{1\leq i\leq k}s_{i}-s_{i-1}>L^{-0.99}
, then for each
j=1,\ldots,k
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{j}<(s_{j}-s_{j-1})L-K(s_{j}-s_{j-1})^{1/2}L^{1/4}\ \Big|\ \sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}>s_{k}L\right)<\exp(-cK^{2}).

The above bound is optimal except for the fact that we require
K>CL^{3/8}
, while it should hold for
K>C
; the loss is due to the non-optimal error term in our tail bound
Theorem

2.13
.
The constraint
\min_{1\leq i\leq k}s_{i}-s_{i-1}>L^{-0.99}
is due to applying
Theorem

2.13
and
Corollary

6.2
. We also mention the related result
[
Liu22b
, Theorem 1.1]
that implies that, conditional on
\mathcal{L}(0,0;0,1)=L
, the weight of the geodesic up to height
s
is
sL
plus a random term on scale
L^{1/4}
which, when scaled to be unit order, converges to a Gaussian random variable.

Proof of Lemma
6.3
.

By the independence of these
\mathcal{L}_{j}^{\beta}
, without loss of generality, we prove the estimate for
j=k
.

Let
Y=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}
, and
X=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{k}-(s_{k}-s_{k-1})L
. Then the probability in the LHS equals

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=K(s_{k}-s_{k-1})^{1/2}L^{1/4}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(X\in-\ell+[-1,0]\ \Big|\ \sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}>s_{k}L\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{\ell=K(s_{k}-s_{k-1})^{1/2}L^{1/4}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}>s_{k}L\ \Big|\ X\in-\ell+[-1,0]\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(X\in-\ell+[-1,0]\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}>s_{k}L\right)}

(6.1)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sum_{\ell=K(s_{k}-s_{k-1})^{1/2}L^{1/4}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(Y>s_{k-1}L+\ell\ \Big|\ X\in-\ell+[-1,0]\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(X\in-\ell+[-1,0]\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}>s_{k}L\right)}.

We can lower bound the denominator by
\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}>(s_{i}-s_{i-1})L)
, which, by
Theorem

2.13
, is
>\exp\left(-\frac{4}{3}s_{k}L^{3/2}-Cs_{k}^{1/2}L^{3/4}\right)
.
For the numerator, note that
Y
is independent of
X
, so the summand indexed by
\ell
in (
6.1
) can be upper bounded, using
Corollary

6.2
and Theorem
2.13
by

\displaystyle\exp\left(-\tfrac{4}{3}s_{k-1}^{-1/2}\left(s_{k-1}L+\ell\right)^{3/2}-\tfrac{4}{3}(s_{k}-s_{k-1})^{-1/2}\left((s_{k}-s_{k-1})L-\ell\right)^{3/2}+Cs_{k}^{1/2}L^{3/4}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\tfrac{4}{3}s_{k}L^{3/2}-c\ell^{2}(s_{k}-s_{k-1})^{-1}L^{-1/2}+Cs_{k}^{1/2}L^{3/4}\right).

Substituting this into (
6.1
) and using that
K>C_{1}L^{3/8}
for a large enough constant
C_{1}
(so that
cK^{2}
is much larger than
s_{k}^{1/2}L^{3/4}
) yields the claim.
∎

The following lemma provides control on the lower tail of the sum
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}
, conditional on the upper tail of
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}
.

Lemma 6.4
.

Assume that
\min_{1\leq i\leq k}s_{i}-s_{i-1}>t_{0}
for some
t_{0}>0
.
For any
L>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{\vec{y}:\|\vec{y}\|_{\infty}\leq L^{-1/4}\log L}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}<L-L^{5/8}\log L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)\\
<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}),

where the constants
C,c
may depend on
t_{0}
.

Proof.

We assume that
L
is large enough since otherwise the conclusion follows obviously.

We start with
\beta=1
. We first claim that, conditional on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
, it holds with probability at least
1-C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})
that
\sup_{\vec{y}:\|\vec{y}\|_{\infty}\leq L^{-1/4}\log L}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}>L-C\log L
. Indeed, suppose that this inequality does not hold. In the
\beta=1
case, we know from the quenched transversal fluctuation estimate Lemma
5.4
that with conditional probability at least
1-C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})
,

\displaystyle\exp(L)

\displaystyle<\left(1-e^{-cL^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}}\right)^{-1}\int_{[-L^{-1/4}\log L,L^{-1/4}\log L]^{k-1}}\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\right)\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\,\mathrm{d}y_{i},

where
y_{k}=0
in the integral.
If
\sup_{\vec{y}:\|\vec{y}\|_{\infty}\leq L^{-1/4}\log L}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}<L-C\log L
, the RHS is upper bounded by

\displaystyle CL^{1/2}(\log L)^{-2}(2L^{-1/4}\log L)^{k}\exp\left(L-C\log L\right)\ll\exp(L),

which is a contradiction.

In the
\beta=\infty
case, by Lemma
5.2
, with conditional probability at least
1-C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})
it holds that
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}=\sup_{\vec{y}:\|\vec{y}\|_{\infty}\leq L^{-1/4}\log L}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}
, which implies our claim since we have conditioned on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
.

Next, we know from
Lemma

3.1
that, with (unconditional) probability at least
1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2}\log L)
,

\sup_{\|\vec{y}\|_{\infty}\leq L^{-1/4}\log L}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left|\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}-\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,s_{i-1};0,s_{i})\right|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}L^{3/4}(\log L)^{1/2}\cdot(L^{-1/4}\log L)^{1/2}=\tfrac{1}{2}L^{5/8}\log L.

By Theorem
2.13
we know
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)>\exp(-CL^{3/2})
, the previous bound also holds conditionally on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2}\log L)
. This completes the proof.
∎

7.
Concentration of polymers

In this section, we mainly work with polymers, i.e., set
\beta=1
. We prove the following fact: the polymer measure at a given height
s
is a Dirac mass spread out over an
L^{-1/2}
interval around a random location.
More specifically, we define

\pi(s)=\argmax_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x,s)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s;0,1),

for any
s\in(0,1)
.
Note that this is a function purely of the environment.
The main result of this section asserts that under the polymer measure
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}
, the polymer path at each height in
[L^{-5/8},1-L^{-5/8}]
stays within
O(L^{-1/2}\log L)
of
\pi
with high probability.

Proposition 7.1
.

There exists
M_{0}>0
such that for any
L\geq 2
,
M>M_{0}
and
s\in[L^{-5/8},1-L^{-5/8}]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left(|\Gamma_{0}(s)-\pi(s)|>ML^{-1/2}\log L\right)>L^{-2M}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)>L\right)<C\exp(-c(\log(L))^{2}).

In the rest of this section, we shall always take
s\in[L^{-5/8},1-L^{-5/8}]
.
For the convenience of notations, we adopt the notation
s_{1}=s
and
s_{2}=1-s
,
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}(x)=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x,s)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}(x)=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s;0,1)
, and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)
.

7.1.
Global to two-segments conditioning

In the upcoming proof of Proposition
7.1
, it will be useful to go from conditioning on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
to conditioning on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}(x)\in(h_{i},h_{i}+\,\mathrm{d}h_{i})
for each
i=1,2
, with
x,h_{1},h_{2}
belonging to a set of nice values. The following is the general statement that allows us to do this.

Let
\mathrm{Val}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
be defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{Val}=\left\{(h_{1},h_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:L-(\log L)^{2}<h_{1}+h_{2}<L+(\log L)^{2},h_{i}>s_{i}L-s_{i}^{1/2}L^{5/8}\log L\text{ for }i=1,2\right\}.

Lemma 7.2

(Global to two-segments conditioning)
.

For any
L>0
,
0<K\leq\log L
, and any event
A
,

\mathbb{P}\left(A\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in L^{-2}\mathbb{Z},|x|\leq KL^{-1/4}\end{subarray}}\sup_{(h_{1},h_{2})\in\mathrm{Val}}\mathbb{P}\left(A\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}(x)\in(h_{i},h_{i}+\,\mathrm{d}h_{i}),i=1,2\right)\\
\times C\exp(C(\log L)^{2}L^{1/2})+C\exp(-cK^{2}).

Proof.

We assume that
K>1
since otherwise the conclusion follows trivially by setting
C
large.

We observe from Lemma
5.4
that, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cK^{2})
, conditionally on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
,

\displaystyle\int_{[-KL^{-1/4},KL^{-1/4}]}\exp\left((\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2})(x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\exp\left(-\tfrac{1}{2}K^{2}L^{-1/2}\right)\right)\exp(L)

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(L+\log\left(\tfrac{1}{2}K^{2}L^{-1/2}\right)\right),

which implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{[-KL^{-1/4},KL^{-1/4}]}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}\geq L-\log L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)>1-C\exp(-cK^{2}).

This with unconditional local fluctuation estimates (
Proposition

2.16
) implies that

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{x\in L^{-2}\mathbb{Z},|x|\leq KL^{-1/4}}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2})(x)\leq L-(\log L)^{2}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-cL^{2}(\log L)^{4})+C\exp(-cK^{2}).

Also, for each
|x|\leq KL^{-1/4}
,
by
Theorem

4.1
and
Lemma

6.1
,

\mathbb{P}\left((\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2})(x)\geq L+(\log L)^{2}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}L^{1/2});

and by proportionality (
Lemma

6.3
), we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}(x)\leq s_{i}L-s_{i}^{1/2}L^{5/8}\log L,\;(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2})(x)>L-(\log L)^{2}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)\\
<\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}(x)\leq s_{i}L-s_{i}^{1/2}L^{5/8}\log L\ \Big|\ (\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2})(x)>L-(\log L)^{2}\right)\frac{\mathbb{P}\left((\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2})(x)>L-(\log L)^{2}\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)}\\
<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}L^{3/4}),

for each
i=1,2
, where the ratio is bounded using
Corollary

6.2
and
Theorem

2.13
.
Combining the above three estimates, we see that with probability
>1-C\exp(-cK^{2})
, there exists one
x\in L^{-2}\mathbb{Z},|x|\leq KL^{-1/4}
, such that
\mathrm{ValueCtrl}_{x}
holds; where

\displaystyle\mathrm{ValueCtrl}_{x}=\left\{L-(\log L)^{2}<(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2})(x)<L+(\log L)^{2},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}(x)>s_{i}L-s_{i}^{1/2}L^{5/8}\log L\text{ for }i=1,2\right\}.

In other words, we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{ValueCtrl}^{c}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)<C\exp(-cK^{2}),

where
\mathrm{ValueCtrl}=\cup_{x\in L^{-2}\mathbb{Z},|x|\leq KL^{-1/4}}\mathrm{ValueCtrl}_{x}
.
Now we have

\mathbb{P}(A\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)<\mathbb{P}(A\cap\mathrm{ValueCtrl}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)+C\exp(-cK^{2}).

Note that the first term in the RHS is bounded by

\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in L^{-2}\mathbb{Z},|x|\leq KL^{-1/4}\end{subarray}}\sup_{(h_{1},h_{2})\in\mathrm{Val}}\mathbb{P}\left(A\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}(x)\in(h_{i},h_{i}+\,\mathrm{d}h_{i}),i=1,2\right)\frac{\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{ValueCtrl})}{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)}.

By
Lemma

6.1
and
Theorem

4.1
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{ValueCtrl})\leq\sum_{x\in L^{-2}\mathbb{Z},|x|\leq KL^{-1/4}}\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{ValueCtrl}_{x})\\
\leq\sum_{x\in L^{-2}\mathbb{Z},|x|\leq KL^{-1/4}}\mathbb{P}((\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2})(x)>L-(\log L)^{2})<C\exp(C(\log L)^{2}L^{1/2})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L).

Combining the last three displays leads to the conclusion.
∎

7.2.
Random location and concentration

We next use
Lemma

7.2
to control the location of
\pi(s)
, as well as prove
Proposition

7.1
.

The next result asserts that
\pi(s)
is of order
L^{-1/4}
.

Proposition 7.3
.

For any
L
large enough,
s\in[L^{-5/8},1-L^{-5/8}]
, and
0<K\leq\log(L)
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(|\pi(s)|>KL^{-1/4}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-cK^{2}).

The general idea to prove
Proposition

7.3
is to (1) upper bound
\max_{(-KL^{-1/4},KL^{-1/4})^{c}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}
conditional on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}(x)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}(x)
for some
x=O(KL^{-1/4})
, using
Corollary

2.21
; and (2) connect the conditioning
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
and the conditioning on the values of
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}(x)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}(x)
, using
Lemma

7.2
.

Proof of
Proposition

7.3
.

We assume that
K>1
since otherwise the conclusion follows trivially by setting
C
large.

As in the proof of
Lemma

7.2
, we have

(7.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}\geq L-\log L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)>1-C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}).

We also have

(7.2)

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{(-(s_{1}\wedge s_{2})L^{1/2}/2,(s_{1}\wedge s_{2})L^{1/2}/2)^{c}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}\geq(1-\tfrac{1}{10}(s_{1}\wedge s_{2}))L\right)\\
<C\exp(-c(s_{1}\wedge s_{2})L^{3/2})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L),

by upper bounding the LHS using
Corollary

6.2
plus shear invariance and a union bound, and lower bounding
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)
using
Theorem

2.13
.
Combining (
7.1
) and (
7.2
) implies that

(7.3)

\mathbb{P}\left(|\pi(s)|>KL^{-1/4}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})\\
+\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{[-\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}\wedge s_{2})L^{1/2},-KL^{-1/4}]\cup[KL^{-1/4},\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}\wedge s_{2})L^{1/2}]}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}\geq L-\log L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right).

By
Corollary

2.21
with
a=KL^{-1/4}
, for any
|x|\leq KL^{-1/4}/2
, and
(h_{1},h_{2})\in\mathrm{Val}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\max_{[-\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}\wedge s_{2})L^{1/2},-KL^{-1/4}]\cup[KL^{-1/4},\frac{1}{2}(s_{1}\wedge s_{2})L^{1/2}]}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}\geq L-\log L\\
\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}(x)\in(h_{i},h_{i}+\,\mathrm{d}h_{i}),i=1,2\Bigg)<C\exp(-cKL^{3/4})

Thus by
Lemma

7.2
, the second term in the RHS of (
7.3
) can be bounded by
C\exp(-cK^{2})
, and the conclusion follows.
∎

We next finish proving the polymer concentration result.

Proof of Proposition
7.1
.

Consider the event
\mathrm{Tent}
defined by

\mathrm{Tent}:=\left\{\max_{|z-\pi(s)|\geq ML^{-1/2}\log L}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}(z)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}(z)-(L-2L^{1/2}|z-\pi(s)|)<0\right\}

To understand this definition, recall that we expect each of
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}
to essentially adopt tent shapes under the conditioning
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
, where the tents each have slope approximately
\pm 2L^{1/2}
. Thus the sum
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}
can be expected to be a line of slope
\pm 4L^{1/2}
up to random fluctuations; in the definition of the event, we have reduced the slope magnitude by
1/2
for the benefit of ignoring the random fluctuation.

Now, on
\mathrm{Tent}
,

\displaystyle\int_{|x-\pi(s)|\geq ML^{-1/2}\log L}\exp\left((\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2})(x)\right)\,\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{|x-\pi(s)|\geq ML^{-1/2}\log L}\exp\left(L-2L^{1/2}|x-\pi(s)|\right)\,\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=2\int_{ML^{-1/2}\log L}^{\infty}\exp\left(L-2L^{1/2}x\right)\,\,\mathrm{d}x=L^{-1/2}\exp\left(L-2M\log L\right),

which implies that, on
\mathrm{Tent}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left(|\Gamma_{0}(s)-\pi(s)|>ML^{-1/2}\log L\right)<L^{-2M}.

Thus our task is now to upper bound
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Tent}^{c}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)
.
We note that by
Proposition

7.3
,

(7.4)

\mathbb{P}\left(|\pi(s)|>\log(L)L^{-1/4}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}).

Then it remains to upper bound
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Tent}^{c}\cap\{|\pi(s)|\leq\log(L)L^{-1/4}\}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)
.

We define the event
\mathrm{Tent}_{+}
by

\mathrm{Tent}_{+}:=\left\{\max_{|z|\geq(s_{1}\wedge s_{2})L^{1/2}/3}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}(z)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}(z)-(L-3L^{1/2}|z|)<0\right\}.

Similar to (
7.2
) in the proof of
Proposition

7.3
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Tent}_{+}^{c})<C\exp(-c(s_{1}\wedge s_{2})L^{3/2})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L),

by upper bounding the LHS using
Corollary

6.2
plus shear invariance and a union bound, and lower bounding
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)
using
Theorem

2.13
.
Then we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Tent}_{+}^{c}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)<C\exp(-c(s_{1}\wedge s_{2})L^{3/2})
.
It now suffices to upper bound
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Tent}^{c}\cap\mathrm{Tent}_{+}\cap\{|\pi(s)|\leq\log(L)L^{-1/4}\}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)
, and we apply
Lemma

7.2
for this.

Take
x\in L^{-2}\mathbb{Z}
,
|x|\leq\log(L)L^{-1/4}
and
(h_{1},h_{2})\in\mathrm{Val}
, and consider

(7.5)

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{Tent}^{c}\cap\mathrm{Tent}_{+}\cap\{|\pi(s)|\leq\log(L)L^{-1/4}\}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}(x)\in(h_{i},h_{i}+\,\mathrm{d}h_{i}),i=1,2\right).

Assuming that for each
i=1,2
,
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}(x)\in(h_{i},h_{i}+\,\mathrm{d}h_{i})
, and

\max_{ML^{-1/2}\log(L)/5\leq|y|\leq(s_{1}\wedge s_{2})L^{1/2}/2}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}(x+y)+\tfrac{3}{2}|y|(h_{i}/s_{i})^{1/2}\leq h_{i},

and
\mathrm{Tent}_{+}\cap\{|\pi(s)|\leq\log(L)L^{-1/4}\}
,
we must have
|\pi(s)-x|<ML^{-1/2}\log(L)/5
, and
\mathrm{Tent}
holds.
Therefore by
Corollary

2.21
(with
a=ML^{-1/2}\log L
), we have that (
7.5
) is bounded by
C\exp(-cML^{1/2}\log L)+C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
.
Then by
Lemma

7.2
, and taking
M_{0}
large enough, we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Tent}^{c}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)<C\exp(-cL^{1/2}\log L)+C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})
. Thus the conclusion follows.
∎

8.
Tightness for polymers

We prove the
\beta=1
case of
Proposition

5.1
in this section.
We also denote
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)
for simplicity of notations.

The main task is to prove the following two points estimate, which refines
Lemma

5.6
.

Proposition 8.1
.

For all
L\geq 2
,
0<s<t<1
and
K>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left(|\Gamma_{0}(s)-\Gamma_{0}(t)|>K(t-s)^{1/11}L^{-1/4}\right)>L^{-K}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-c(K\wedge\log L)^{2}).

Compared to the zero temperature setting (i.e.,
Proposition

5.3
), we weaken our demand to a Hölder
\frac{1}{11}-
bound instead of
\frac{1}{2}-
, due to technical reasons which will be clear from its proof.

Proposition

8.1
immediately implies the following.

Corollary 8.2
.

For all
L\geq 2
,
K>0
and
0<s<t<1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left(|\Gamma_{0}(s)-\Gamma_{0}(t)|>K(t-s)^{1/11}L^{-1/4}\right)\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right]<C\exp(-c(K\wedge\log L)^{2}).

Remark 8.3
.

As indicated in the introduction, from these tightness results and the polymer concentration of
Proposition

7.1
, one can define a random backbone
\tilde{\pi}
, by e.g., taking
\tilde{\pi}(s)=\pi(s)
for each
s\in[0,1]\cap L^{-10}\mathbb{Z}
, and linearly interpolate between them.
Then with probability
>1-C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})
,
\Gamma_{0}
is within distance
L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}
from
\tilde{\pi}
at each
s\in[0,1]\cap L^{-10}\mathbb{Z}
, by
Proposition

7.1
; and between any two points,
\Gamma_{0}
can deviate at most
L^{-1}
, by
Corollary

8.2
.
Therefore (with the same probability)
\Gamma_{0}
is within distance
L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}
from the backbone
\tilde{\pi}
throughout
[0,1]
.

Proof of the
\beta=1
case of
Proposition

5.1
.

We recall that tightness on
\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
follows by establishing a uniform modulus of continuity as well as one-point tightness. Since our processes are fixed at 0 at heights 0 and 1, the former also implies the latter, so we only need to establish a uniform modulus of continuity bound.

With
Corollary

8.2
, via a union bound over all
s=2^{-i}j
,
t=2^{-i}(j+1)
,
0<s<t<1
, with
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have the following. For any
K>0
, with probability
>1-C\exp(-c(K\wedge\log L)^{2})
conditional on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
,

\sup_{t_{0}\leq s<t\leq 1-t_{0}}|\Gamma_{0}(s)-\Gamma_{0}(t)|(t-s)^{-1/12}\leq KL^{-1/4}.

Note that the exponent is
\frac{1}{12}
in contrast to the
\frac{1}{11}
present in Corollary
8.2
; this is simply so that the union bound over all the scales can be performed. This completes the proof.
∎

To prove
Proposition

8.1
, the key idea is to upgrade
Lemma

5.6
using the concentration result
Proposition

7.1
.
It allows us to essentially say that (with high probability under
\mathbb{P}
) if the law of
\Gamma_{0}(s)
under
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}
assigns a probability greater than
\varepsilon
(for a carefully chosen small
\varepsilon
) to the event of large transversal fluctuation, it must assign a close to
1
probability to the event of having at least say half of the same transversal fluctuation. The latter event’s probability is bounded by
Lemma

5.6
.
Note that in this argument it is actually not important that the interval around which most of the mass is spread is centered around
\pi(s)
, only that the interval is small.

We start by first upgrading the one-point estimate, at least
L^{-5/8}
away from the ends.

Proposition 8.4
.

For all
K>0
,
L\geq 2
, and
s\in[L^{-5/8},1-L^{-5/8}]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left(|\Gamma_{0}(s)|\geq KL^{-1/4}\right)>L^{-K}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-c(K\wedge\log L)^{2}).

Proof.

We assume that
K
is large enough since otherwise we just choose
C
large to make the estimate hold.
We also assume that
L
is large enough since otherwise the conclusion follows from
Lemma

5.4
.

Let
X(s,K)=\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}(|\Gamma_{0}(s)|\geq KL^{-1/4})
. By Proposition
7.1
, with probability
\geq 1-C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})
conditional on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
we have

\displaystyle X(s,K)

\displaystyle\leq\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left(|\Gamma_{0}(s)-\pi(s)|\geq KL^{-1/2}\log L\right)+\mathbbm{1}_{|\pi(s)|\geq\frac{1}{2}KL^{-1/4}}

\displaystyle\leq L^{-2K}+\mathbbm{1}_{|\pi(s)|\geq\frac{1}{2}KL^{-1/4}},

and

\displaystyle X(s,\tfrac{1}{4}K)

\displaystyle\geq\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left(|\Gamma_{0}(s)-\pi(s)|\leq KL^{-1/2}\log L\right)\mathbbm{1}_{|\pi(s)|\geq\frac{1}{2}KL^{-1/4}}

\displaystyle\geq(1-L^{-2K})\mathbbm{1}_{|\pi(s)|\geq\frac{1}{2}KL^{-1/4}}.

Here we used that
KL^{-1/2}\log L<\frac{1}{4}KL^{-1/4}
.
These two bounds show that

\displaystyle X(s,K)>L^{-2K}\implies|\pi(s)|\geq\tfrac{1}{2}KL^{-1/4}\implies X(s,\tfrac{1}{4}K)\geq 1-L^{-2K},

and thus

\mathbb{P}\left(X(s,K)>L^{-2K}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<\mathbb{P}\left(X(s,\tfrac{1}{4}K)\geq 1-L^{-2K}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)+C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}).

By Lemma
5.4
,
\mathbb{P}\left(X(s,\tfrac{1}{4}K)>\exp(-K^{2}L^{-1/2}/32)\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-cK^{2})
. This completes the proof.
∎

Next, we turn to the two-point estimates. The proof strategies are analogous to what we just saw for the one-point: we combine a cruder estimate coming from shear invariance with the information that the polymer measure is localized on a smaller scale. We initially get the following two-point estimate, which is under the additional constraints that the two points are at least
L^{-5/8}
away from each other, and
L^{-5/8}
away from the ends.
To get
Proposition

8.1
from it, it turns out that the cruder estimate
Lemma

5.6
is sufficient, since we just prove an Hölder
\frac{1}{10}-
bound instead of
\frac{1}{2}-
.

Proposition 8.5
.

For all
K>0
,
L\geq 2
, and
L^{-5/8}\leq s<t\leq 1-L^{-5/8}
,
t-s\geq L^{-5/8}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left(|\Gamma_{0}(s)-\Gamma_{0}(t)|>K(t-s)^{1/3}L^{-1/4}\right)>L^{-K}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-c(K\wedge\log L)^{2}).

We note that the exponent
1/3
can be replaced by any number
<2/5
, due to the exponent of
5/8
(we only need that their product is
<1/4
).

Proof of
Proposition

8.5
.

This proof is very similar to that of
Proposition

8.4
.
Again, we can assume that
L,K
are large enough.
Let
X(s,t,K)=\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left(|\Gamma_{0}(s)-\Gamma_{0}(t)|>K(t-s)^{1/3}L^{-1/4}\right)
. By Proposition
7.1
, with probability
\geq 1-C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})
conditional on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
,

\displaystyle X(s,t,K)

\displaystyle\leq\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left(\max_{r\in\{s,t\}}|\Gamma_{0}(r)-\pi(r)|\geq KL^{-1/2}\log L\right)+\mathbbm{1}_{|\pi(s)-\pi(t)|\geq\frac{1}{2}K(t-s)^{1/3}L^{-1/4}}

\displaystyle\leq 2L^{-2K}+\mathbbm{1}_{|\pi(s)-\pi(t)|\geq\frac{1}{2}K(t-s)^{1/3}L^{-1/4}}

and

\displaystyle X(s,t,\tfrac{1}{4}K)

\displaystyle\geq\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left(\max_{r\in\{s,t\}}|\Gamma_{0}(r)-\pi(r)|\leq KL^{-1/2}\log L\right)\mathbbm{1}_{|\pi(s)-\pi(t)|\geq\frac{1}{2}(t-s)^{1/3}KL^{-1/4}}

\displaystyle\geq(1-2L^{-2K})\mathbbm{1}_{|\pi(s)-\pi(t)|\geq\frac{1}{2}(t-s)^{1/3}KL^{-1/4}}.

Here we used that
KL^{-1/2}\log L<\frac{1}{4}K(t-s)^{1/3}L^{-1/4}
since
t-s>L^{-5/8}
.
These imply that

\displaystyle X(s,t,K)>2L^{-2K}\implies|\pi(s)-\pi(t)|\geq\tfrac{1}{2}K(t-s)^{1/3}L^{-1/4}\implies X(s,t,\tfrac{1}{4}K)\geq 1-2L^{-2K}.

Besides, by
Lemma

5.6
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(X(s,t,\frac{1}{4}K)>\exp(-K^{2}L^{-1/2}/32)\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-cK^{2})
. These complete the proof.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

8.1
.

We assume that
K
is large enough by taking
C
large (if necessary), and assume that
L
is large enough by applying
Lemma

5.6
otherwise.

For the case where
t-s<2L^{-5/8}
,
by
Lemma

5.6
, we can bound the conditional probability in the statement of
Proposition

8.1
by

C\exp(-cK^{2}(t-s)^{-9/11})<C\exp(-cK^{2}),

since
\exp(-\frac{1}{2}K^{2}(t-s)^{-9/11}L^{-1/2})\leq\exp(-cK^{2}L^{1/88})<L^{-K}
.

For the case where
t-s\geq 2L^{-5/8}
, if
s<L^{-5/8}
, for
s_{*}=s
or
L^{-5/8}
, we apply
Lemma

5.4
to get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left[|\Gamma_{0}(s_{*})|\geq K(s_{*}(1-s_{*}))^{1/11}L^{-1/4}\right]>L^{-3K}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-cK^{2}s_{*}^{-9/11}),

since
\exp(-\frac{1}{2}K^{2}(s_{*}(1-s_{*}))^{-9/11}L^{-1/2})<L^{-3K}
.
Therefore we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left(|\Gamma_{0}(s)-\Gamma_{0}(L^{-5/8})|>K(L^{-5/8}-s)^{1/11}L^{-1/4}\right)>L^{-2K}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-cK^{2}).

Similarly, if
t>1-L^{-5/8}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathscr{P}}\left(|\Gamma_{0}(t)-\Gamma_{0}(1-L^{-5/8})|>K(t-1+L^{-5/8})^{1/11}L^{-1/4}\right)>L^{-2K}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-cK^{2}).

Thus we have reduced the problem of
s,t
to the same problem of
s\vee L^{-5/8},t\wedge(1-L^{-5/8})
, and that follows from
Proposition

8.5
.
∎
