The remaining sections are devoted to proving finite dimensional convergence.

9.
Estimates on free energies under conditionings

As indicated in
Section

1.2
, our strategy of deducing finite dimensional limit heavily relies on realizing the conditioning on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)
as conditioning on the existence of peaks at certain heights at certain locations. As such, we will often need estimates on the probability of the existence of such peaks given the global conditioning
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)
, or on the probability of the latter conditioned on the former.
One such estimate (
Lemma

6.4
) has appeared before, and in this section we provide some more refined ones.

Let us introduce the setup, and some notations needed to state these estimates.
We will work under a setting similar to that in
Section

6
.
Namely, we consider
(s_{1},\cdots,s_{k-1})\in\mathring{\Lambda}_{k-1}([0,1])
and
\vec{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k-1}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and denote
s_{0}=x_{0}=x_{k}=0
and
s_{k}=1
.
All the constants within this section can depend on
k
and
(s_{1},\cdots,s_{k-1})
.
For the convenience of notations we adopt the shorthand
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)
and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(s_{i-1},x_{i-1};s_{i},x_{i})
for each
1\leq i\leq k
.
We also write
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}
for the vector
\{\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
.

Below we use
x\approx y
to denote that
x\in y+[0,e^{-L}]
; and for any vector
\vec{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\smash{\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}}
is the event where
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\approx h_{i}
for each
i
.
A main reason for introducing this notation is that we will need to invoke coalescence or Brownian comparison statements (
Proposition

3.4
and
Proposition

3.5
) which do not allow conditioning on exact values.

Take
\vec{a}=(a_{0},\ldots,a_{k})\in[-L^{5/16}\log L,L^{5/16}\log L]^{k-1}
.
For each
i=1,\ldots,k-1
, we write

\pi^{*}(s_{i})=\argmax_{x}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(a_{i-1},s_{i-1};x,s_{i})+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s_{i};a_{i+1},s_{i+1}).

This differs from
\pi
because
\pi(s_{i})
is defined as the maximizer of
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x,s_{i})+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s_{i};0,1)
, while
\pi^{*}(s_{i})
is the maximizer of profiles within certain time strips which are disjoint for different
i
; this disjointness gives some independence which will be useful for the arguments.

We next introduce useful notations generalizing the maximum to positive temperature: for
f:\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\to\mathbb{R}
and a set
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k-1}
,

(9.1)

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\vec{x}\in I}\kern 3.02678pt}{\emaxop_{\vec{x}\in I}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{x}\in I}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{x}\in I}^{(\beta)}}}f=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\log\int_{I}\exp\bigl(f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k-1})\bigr)\,\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\,\mathrm{d}x_{k-1}&\beta=1\\
\displaystyle\max_{\vec{x}\in I}f(x_{1}\cdots\,\mathrm{d}x_{k-1})&\beta=\infty.\end{cases}

We further define the restricted free energy
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[\vec{y},R]
for
R>0
and
\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k-1}
by

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[\vec{y},R]=\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\|\vec{x}-\vec{y}\|_{\infty}\leq R}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{\|\vec{x}-\vec{y}\|_{\infty}\leq R}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\|\vec{x}-\vec{y}\|_{\infty}\leq R}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\|\vec{x}-\vec{y}\|_{\infty}\leq R}^{(\beta)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}.

Let
r_{\beta=1}=1
and
r_{\beta=\infty}=L^{-1/2}
. These are the fluctuation scales of the total free energy conditional on the peak heights and locations.
Let
w_{\beta=1}=L^{-1/2}
and
w_{\beta=\infty}=L^{-1}
. These are the window sizes around each
\pi^{*}(s_{i})
that would affect
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}
, under the upper tail.

The next two statements record the above-mentioned complementary estimates and will be the goal of this section.

Proposition 9.1
.

Take any large enough
L,M
, and
\vec{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\vec{a}\in[-L^{5/16}\log L,L^{5/16}\log L]^{k+1}
,
\vec{x}\in[-L^{5/16}\log L,L^{5/16}\log L]^{k-1}
.
Denote
H=\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}
and assume that

H>L/2
and each
\left|h_{i}-(s_{i}-s_{i-1})H\right|<L^{8/9}
. Then we have

(9.2)

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>H-(k-1)\beta^{-1}\log(2H^{1/2})+Mr_{\beta},\\
\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta}\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}\bigg)<C\exp\left(-cM^{2}L^{1/2}r_{\beta}\right)+C\exp(-cL^{3/2}),

(9.3)

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[\vec{x},L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}]>H-(k-1)\beta^{-1}\log(2H^{1/2})+Mr_{\beta},\\
\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta}\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}\bigg)>c\exp\left(-CM^{2}L^{1/2}r_{\beta}\right)-C\exp(-cL^{3/2}).

Moreover, we also have

(9.4)

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>H-(k-1)\beta^{-1}\log(2H^{1/2})+M\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}\right)<C\exp\left(-cML^{1/2}\right).

The term
(k-1)\log(2H^{1/2})
is meant to be present in the case
\beta=1
and absent in the case
\beta=\infty
, and multiplying the term by
\beta^{-1}
is a convenient notational tool to this effect (though in fact if one were to work out the arguments in the case of general
\beta
, the term would be
(k-1)\beta^{-1}\log(2\beta H^{1/2})
).
The source of the log term for
\beta=1
comes from the fact that
\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\exp(-4H^{1/2}|x|)\,\mathrm{d}x=(2H^{1/2})^{-1}
, which itself is a result that the dominant contribution to the integral is from an interval of scale
L^{-1/2}
around zero. Since on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
the terms in the exponential in the convolution formula for
\exp(\mathcal{L}^{\beta})
are essentially sums of two Brownian bridges with slope
-2H^{1/2}
each, heuristically taking logarithms will yield that
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}
loses
(k-1)\log(2H^{1/2})
compared to the peak height of
H
.

We next give an estimate on the probability of
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}
being much smaller than
L
, conditional on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
and
\pi^{*}
. It can be viewed as a refinement of
Lemma

6.4
.

Proposition 9.2
.

For all large enough
L,M
with
M<L^{0.01}
, and
\vec{x}\in[-L^{5/16}\log L,L^{5/16}\log L]^{k-1}
,
\vec{x}\in[-L^{5/16}\log L,L^{5/16}\log L]^{k-1}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(E_{k,M,w,L}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L,\max_{i=1,\ldots,k}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta}\right)\leq\exp\left(-cM^{2}L^{1/2}r_{\beta}\right),

where

(9.5)

E_{k,M,w,L}=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\in L+(k-1)\beta^{-1}\log(2L^{1/2})+[-L^{8/9},-Mr_{\beta}]\right\}\\
\cap\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}\left\{\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}>(s_{i}-s_{i-1})L-k^{-1}L^{8/9}\right\}.

Proposition
9.2
is proved by invoking Bayes’ theorem and Proposition
9.1
, and we give its proof now; we will return to proving Proposition
9.1
later.
For any vector
\vec{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, denote

\displaystyle A_{\vec{h}}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}\left\{\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\in h_{i}+[0,r_{\beta}]\right\}.

Proof of Proposition
9.2
.

We assume without loss of generality that
M\in\mathbb{N}
. Define
\mathrm{Val}_{M}
by

\mathrm{Val}_{M}=\left\{\vec{h}\in(r_{\beta}\mathbb{Z})^{k}:\parbox[c]{289.07999pt}{\centering$\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}\in L+(k-1)\beta^{-1}\log(2L^{1/2})+[-L^{8/9}-r_{\beta},-Mr_{\beta}]$ \\
$h_{i}\in(s_{i}-s_{i-1})L+[-L^{8/9},L^{8/9}],\quad i=1,\ldots,k$
\@add@centering}\right\}.

By doing a disjoint decomposition and applying Bayes’ theorem,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(E_{k,M,w,L}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L,\max_{i=1,\ldots,k}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sum_{\vec{h}\in\mathrm{Val}_{M}}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L,\max_{i=1,\ldots,k}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta}\ \Big|\ A_{\vec{h}}\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(A_{\vec{h}}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L,\max_{i=1,\ldots,k}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta}\right)}.

We can also decompose the denominator in a similar fashion, but with the sum over all
\vec{h}
. Now for each
\vec{h}\in\mathrm{Val}_{M}
, we wish to bound the ratio

\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L,\max_{i=1,\ldots,k}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta}\ \Big|\ A_{\vec{h}}\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}(A_{\vec{h}})}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L,\max_{i=1,\ldots,k}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta}\ \Big|\ A_{\vec{h}^{\prime}}\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}(A_{\vec{h}^{\prime}})},

where
\vec{h}^{\prime}\in(r_{\beta}\mathbb{Z})^{k}
is defined as follows: for each
i=2,\ldots,k
,
h_{i}^{\prime}=h_{i}
, while
\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}^{\prime}\in L+(k-1)\beta^{-1}\log(2L^{1/2})+[0,1]
,

h_{1}^{\prime}-h_{1}\in\lfloor h_{1}^{\prime}-h_{1}\rfloor+[0,r_{\beta})
.

By
Theorem

4.1
we have

\frac{\mathbb{P}(A_{\vec{h}})}{\mathbb{P}(A_{\vec{h}^{\prime}})}<C\exp(C(h_{1}^{\prime}-h_{1})L^{1/2}),

and by
Proposition

9.1
we have

\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L,\max_{i=1,\ldots,k}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta}\ \Big|\ A_{\vec{h}}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L,\max_{i=1,\ldots,k}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta}\ \Big|\ A_{\vec{h}^{\prime}}\right)}<C\exp(-c(h_{1}^{\prime}-h_{1})^{2}L^{1/2}r_{\beta})+C\exp(-cL^{3/2}).

By combining these two estimates, we can now bound the ratio as desired.
Then by summing over all
\vec{h}\in\mathrm{Val}_{M}
the conclusion follows.
∎

We next give the proof of
Proposition

9.1
.

9.1.
k=2
setting

The basic step is to prove the following
k=2
version: from this, we can obtain the general
k
-version by invoking coalescence (Proposition
3.4
) to break down the case of general
k
to a collection of
k=2
cases.

The notations within this subsection are slightly different, and we setup now. We take large enough
L>0
,
h_{1}
and
h_{2}=\Theta(L)
,
0<s_{1},s_{2}<1
,
|x_{*}|\leq 2L^{5/16}\log L
.
All the constants in this section can depend on
s_{1}\wedge s_{2}
.
We write
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}=(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x_{*},s_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{*},s_{1};0,s_{1}+s_{2}))
.
For any
R>0
we denote

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[x_{*},R]=\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{|x-x_{*}|\leq R}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{|x-x_{*}|\leq R}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{|x-x_{*}|\leq R}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{|x-x_{*}|\leq R}^{(\beta)}}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x,s_{1})+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s_{1};0,s_{1}+s_{2})

and we write
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[R]=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[0,R]
.

We also denote
\lambda=(s_{1}^{-1/2}h_{1}^{1/2}+s_{2}^{-1/2}h_{2}^{1/2})
to be the first order of the slope of the sum
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x,s_{1})+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s_{1};0,s_{1}+s_{2})
under the conditioning that
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
. We take some
x_{-}
,
x_{+}
with
|x_{-}|,|x_{+}|\leq 2L^{5/16}\log L
and denote

\pi^{*}(s_{1})=\argmax\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{-},0;\cdot,s_{1})+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(\cdot,s_{1};x_{+},s_{1}+s_{2}).

Lemma 9.3
.

Denote
W=10^{-6}(h_{1}s_{1})^{1/2}\wedge(h_{2}s_{2})^{1/2}
and assume
h_{1},h_{2}=\Theta(L)
.
In the case of
\beta=1
, for any
M>L^{1/16}\log L
we have

(9.6)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[W]>h_{1}+h_{2}-\beta^{-1}\log(\lambda)+ML^{-1/4},|\pi^{*}(s_{1})-x_{*}|\leq w_{\beta}\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}\Big)\\
<C\exp(-cM^{2})+C\exp(-cL^{3/2}),

and for any
M>0
,

(9.7)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[x_{*},L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}]>h_{1}+h_{2}-\beta^{-1}\log(\lambda)+ML^{-1/4},|\pi^{*}(s_{1})-x_{*}|\leq w_{\beta}\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}\Big)\\
>c\exp(-CM^{2})-C\exp(-cL^{3/2}).

In the case of
\beta=\infty
, the first two bounds hold for any
M>0
, after (1) replacing
ML^{-1/4}
with
ML^{-1/2}
in both; (2) in the lower bound, replacing
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[x_{*},L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}]
with
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[x_{*},L^{-1}]
.

Moreover, for both
\beta=1
and
\beta=\infty
, and
M
large enough, we have

(9.8)

\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[W]>h_{1}+h_{2}-\beta^{-1}\log(\lambda)+M\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}\Big)<C\exp(-cML^{1/2}).

Proof.

The general idea of this proof is to invoke
Lemma

2.19
, then do computations of Brownian motions.

We can assume that
M
is large enough since otherwise the estimates trivially hold.
For the convenience of notations, we denote
S(x)=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x,s_{1})+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s_{1};0,s_{1}+s_{2})
, and write
B(x)=S(x_{*}+x)-S(x_{*})+2\lambda|x|
.
In light of
Lemma

2.19
, we shall think of
B(x)
as a Brownian motion in the interval
[-CL^{-1/2}\log L,CL^{-1/2}\log L]
.

Positive temperature.

We first consider the case of
\beta=1
(where
w_{\beta=1}=L^{-1/2}
), and turn to the zero temperature (
\beta=\infty
) case later.

Upper bound.

By the convolution formula,

(9.9)

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[W]=\log\int_{-W}^{W}\exp(S(x))\,\mathrm{d}x.

Since
h_{1},h_{2}=\Theta(L)
and
|x_{*}|,|x_{-}|,|x_{+}|\leq 2L^{5/16}\log L
, by
Corollary

2.21
, conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cM^{2})
,

(9.10)

S(x)<h_{1}+h_{2}-2\lambda|x-x_{*}|+C|x-x_{*}|L^{5/16}\log L+ML^{-1/4},

for any
|x-x_{*}|\leq L^{-1/2}
, and

(9.11)

S(x)<h_{1}+h_{2}-2\lambda|x-x_{*}|+C|x-x_{*}|L^{5/16}\log L+M|x-x_{*}|^{1/2}(|\log(|x-x_{*}|LM^{-2})|+1),

for any
x
with
|x-x_{*}|\geq L^{-1/2}
,
|x|\leq W
.

To get (
9.8
), we note that by plugging (
9.10
) and (
9.11
) into (
9.9
), we have that
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[W]<h_{1}+h_{2}+\log(\lambda^{-1})+CM^{2}L^{-1/2}
when
M>L^{1/4}
.
(Note that the RHS of (
9.11
) is maximized when
|x-x_{*}|
is of order
M^{2}/L
.)
Relabeling
M
completes the proof.

To get (
9.6
),
we note that with probability
>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
, the event
|\pi^{*}(s_{1})-x_{*}|\leq L^{-1/2}
implies that

S(x)<h_{1}+h_{2}+ML^{-1/4}+C\exp(-cL)
for all
|x|\leq W
.
Plugging the minimum of this bound and (
9.11
) into (
9.9
), we get that
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[W]<h_{1}+h_{2}+\log(\lambda^{-1})+CML^{-1/4}+CL^{-3/16}\log L
. Using that
M>L^{1/16}\log L
the estimate (
9.6
) follows.

Lower bound.

As the comparison in
Lemma

2.19
is for some interval with length of order
L^{1/2}
, we need to do a truncation for
\pi^{*}(s_{1})
.
Namely, we claim

(9.12)

\mathbb{P}(|\pi^{*}(s_{1})|>W\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h})<C\exp(-cL^{3/2}).

Indeed, from
Theorem

2.13
and
Proposition

2.16
, and
Proposition

3.4
, we can deduce that

\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{|x|>W}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{-},0;x,s_{1})\geq\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{-},0;x_{*},s_{1}),\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}\approx h_{1}\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}\approx h_{1})}<C\exp(-cL^{3/2}),

\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{|x|>W}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s_{1};x_{+},s_{1}+s_{2})\geq\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{*},s_{1};x_{+},s_{1}+s_{2}),\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}\approx h_{2}\right)}{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}\approx h_{2})}<C\exp(-cL^{3/2}).

These together imply (
9.12
).

Now by definition

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[x_{*},L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}]=\log\int_{-L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}}^{L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}}\exp(S(x_{*}+x))\,\mathrm{d}x

(9.13)

\displaystyle=S(x_{*})+\log\int_{-L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}}^{L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}}\exp\left(-2\lambda|x|+B(x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Define events
A_{M}
and
E
as

\begin{split}A_{M}&=\left\{\min_{|x|\in[L^{-1/2}/4,L^{-1/2}/2]}B(x)\geq ML^{-1/4}\right\}\cap\left\{\min_{|x|\leq L^{-1/2}/4}B(x)\geq-L^{-1/4}\right\}\\
&\qquad\cap\left\{B(x)\geq-|x|^{1/2}\log(|x|L^{1/2})\text{ for all }|x|\in[L^{-1/2}/2,L^{-1/2}\log L]\right\},\\
E&=\left\{\max_{|x|\geq L^{-1/2},|x+x_{*}|\leq W}B(x)-2\lambda|x|<\max_{|x|\leq L^{-1/2}}B(x)-2\lambda|x|-C\exp(-cL)\right\}.\end{split}

If
B
were replaced by a two-sided Brownian motion with bounded drift, the probability of
A_{M}\cap E
would be
>c\exp(-CM^{2})
, by standard Brownian motion computations.
Then by
Lemma

2.19
,

\mathbb{P}(A_{M}\cap E\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h})>c\exp(-CM^{2})(1-C\exp(-cL))-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})\\
>c\exp(-CM^{2})-C\exp(-cL^{3/2}).

Assuming
A_{M}\cap E
, we can lower bound (
9.1
) by
h_{1}+h_{2}+\log(\lambda^{-1})+cML^{-1/4}-CL^{-1/4}
;
and
|\pi^{*}(s_{1})|\leq L^{-1/2}
outside an event with probability
<C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
, by
Proposition

3.4
.
Noting that
M
is taken large enough, together with (
9.12
), the lower bound (
9.7
) follows.

These complete the proof of the lemma in the
\beta=1
case. We now turn to the
\beta=\infty
case.

Zero temperature:
We now need to bound the maximum of
S
instead of its integral. For the upper bound, since
h_{1},h_{2}=\Theta(L)
and
|x_{*}|,|x_{-}|,|x_{+}|\leq 2L^{5/16}\log L
, by
Corollary

2.21
, conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cM^{2})
,

S(x)<h_{1}+h_{2}-2\lambda|x-x_{*}|+C|x-x_{*}|L^{5/16}\log L+ML^{-1/2},

for any
|x-x_{*}|\leq L^{-1}
,
and

S(x)<h_{1}+h_{2}-2\lambda|x-x_{*}|+C|x-x_{*}|L^{5/16}\log L+M|x-x_{*}|^{1/2}(|\log(|x-x_{*}|LM^{-2})|+1),

for any
x
with
|x-x_{*}|\geq L^{-1}
,
|x|\leq W
.
Then
S(x)
in
[-W,W]
is at most
h_{1}+h_{2}+CM^{2}L^{-1/2}
(note that the RHS of the previous display is maximized when
|x-x_{*}|
is of order
M^{2}/L
), and we get (
9.8
), by relabeling
M
.

Under the additional assumption that
|\pi^{*}(s_{1})-x_{*}|\leq L^{-1}
(note that
w_{\beta=\infty}=L^{-1}
), by
Proposition

3.4
, we have that
S(x)
in
[-W,W]
is at most
h_{1}+h_{2}+ML^{-1/2}
outside another event of probability
<C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
. Thus we get (
9.6
).

For the lower bound, we note that (
9.12
) still holds with the same proof. Then by
Proposition

3.4
, we just need to consider the probability of

\max_{|x|\leq L^{-1}}B(x)\geq ML^{-1/2},\quad\left|\argmax_{|x+x_{*}|\leq W}B(x)-2\lambda|x|\right|\leq L^{-1}.

Again by
Lemma

2.19
, conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
, the above happens with probability
>c\exp(-CM^{2})-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Below we return to using the notations defined before this subsection, i.e., at the beginning of Section
9
.

9.2.
Proof of
Proposition

9.1

Note that (with
y_{0}=y_{k}=0
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}=\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{k-1}}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{y_{1},\ldots,y_{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}\left(y_{i-1},s_{i-1};y_{i},s_{i}\right).

We want to simplify the RHS using coalescence (Proposition
3.4
). Since we only know that coalescence holds up to a distance of order
L^{1/2}
, we have to first argue that we can restrict the
\max^{(\beta)}
to be over an interval centered at 0 of size much smaller than
L^{1/2}
, with high probability conditionally on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
.

For notational convenience let, for
R>0
and
i=1,\ldots,k-1
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}[x,R]=\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{|y-x|\leq R}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{|y-x|\leq R}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{|y-x|\leq R}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{|y-x|\leq R}^{(\beta)}}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{i-1},s_{i-1};y,s_{i})+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(y,s_{i};x_{i+1},s_{i+1}).

Restricting the interval and coalescence:
Let
\delta=10^{-6}\min_{i=1,\ldots,k}(s_{i}-s_{i-1})
.
Let
\mathcal{E}_{0}
denote the event that

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}\leq\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[\vec{0},\delta L^{1/2}]+C\exp(-cL),

where
\vec{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k-1}
is the vector with every entry being
0
.
Then we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{c}\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h})<\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{c},\mathcal{L}>H-C\log L\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h})+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}\leq H-C\log L\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}).

The second term in the RHS is bounded by
C\exp(-cL^{2})
by
Lemma

6.1
and
Theorem

2.13
; and the first term is bounded by

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{c}\mid\mathcal{L}>H-C\log L)\frac{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}>H-C\log L)}{\mathbb{P}(\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h})}.

By
Lemma

5.4
, we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{c}\mid\mathcal{L}>H)<C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
; and the ratio is bounded by
C\exp(CL^{25/18})
using
Theorem

2.13
(and the assumption that
\vec{x}\in[-L^{5/16}\log L,L^{5/16}\log L]^{k-1}
, and that each
\left|h_{i}-(s_{i}-s_{i-1})H\right|<L^{8/9}
).
Then we conclude that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{c}\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h})<C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
.

By the convolution formula, this with
Proposition

3.4
implies that
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Coal}\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h})>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
, where the coalescence event
\mathrm{Coal}
is defined by

\mathrm{Coal}=\biggl\{\mathcal{L}^{\beta}\in\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}[0,\delta L^{1/2}]-\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}+[-C\exp(-cL),C\exp(-cL)]\biggr\}.

Upper bound:
Under
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
, the sum
\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\in\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}h_{i}+[0,(k-2)e^{-L}]
. Therefore, assuming
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
and
\mathrm{Coal}
,

\displaystyle\Bigl\{\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>H-(k-1)\beta^{-1}\log(2H^{1/2})+Mr_{\beta}\Bigr\}

\displaystyle\subseteq\Biggl\{\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}[0,\delta L^{1/2}]\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}(h_{i}+h_{i+1})-(k-1)\beta^{-1}\log(2H^{1/2})+Mr_{\beta}-C\exp(-cL)\Biggr\}.

Let
P_{i}
be the probability of

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}[0,\delta L^{1/2}]\geq h_{i}+h_{i+1}-\beta^{-1}\log(2H^{1/2})+k^{-1}M,

conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
.
Let
P_{i}^{\prime}
be the probability of

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}[0,\delta L^{1/2}]\geq h_{i}+h_{i+1}-\beta^{-1}\log(2H^{1/2})+k^{-1}Mr_{\beta},

and that
|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta}
, conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
.
By a union bound, it suffices to upper bound
\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}P_{i}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}P_{i}^{\prime}
.

For (
9.4
),
we can then invoke (
9.8
) in
Lemma

9.3
(using shear and translation invariance properties) to obtain an upper bound on each
P_{i}
.
Similarly, for (
9.2
) we invoke (
9.6
) in
Lemma

9.3
to obtain an upper bound on
P_{i}^{\prime}
.
We note that, since
|h_{i}-(s_{i}-s_{i-1})H|<L^{8/9}
, it follows that
\log((s_{i}-s_{i-1})^{-1/2}h_{i}^{1/2}+(s_{i+1}-s_{i})^{-1/2}h_{i+1}^{1/2})=\log(2H^{1/2})+O(L^{-1/9})
, while
\beta^{-1}L^{-1/9}<r_{\beta}
. This completes the proof.

Lower bound:

By the convolution formula and
Proposition

3.4
, conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[\vec{x},L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}]

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}[x_{i},L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}]-\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}-C\exp(-cL)

(9.14)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}[x_{i},L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}]-\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}h_{i}-C\exp(-cL).

Take
2\leq i\leq k-1
.
Consider the processes
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{i-1},s_{i-1};\cdot,s_{i})
and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(\cdot,s_{i-1};x_{i},s_{i})
conditional on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\approx h_{i}
, as a measure on
\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R})^{2}
.
By
Proposition

3.5
, it can be replaced by the product measure of its marginals (up to an error of
C\exp(-cL)
), on an event with probability
>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
, with Radon-Nikodym derivative bounded between
1-C\exp(-cL)
and
1+C\exp(-cL)
.
By
Lemma

9.3
(plus shear and translation invariance properties),
we can lower bound the probability of

(9.15)

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}[x_{i},L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}]\geq h_{i}+h_{i+1}-\beta^{-1}\log(2H^{1/2})+Mr_{\beta},|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta},

conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
, by
c\exp(-CM^{2}L^{1/2}r_{\beta})-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
.
(Here we also use that

\log((s_{i}-s_{i-1})^{-1/2}h_{i}^{1/2}+(s_{i+1}-s_{i})^{-1/2}h_{i+1}^{1/2})=\log(2H^{1/2})+O(L^{-1/9})

and
\beta^{-1}L^{-1/9}<r_{\beta}
.)
By the above coupling to independence, the probability of (
9.15
) for each
i
, conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
, is also
>c\exp(-CM^{2}L^{1/2}r_{\beta})-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
.
Plugging these into (
9.14
) finishes the proof of (
9.3
).
∎

10.
Global and segment maximizers

In
Proposition

7.1
we have shown that (conditional on the upper tail) the polymer
\Gamma_{0}
at any
s\in(0,1)
is close to the maximizer
\pi(s)
, whose definition we recall is

\pi(s)=\argmax_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x,s)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s;0,1).

In zero temperature, we also use
\pi
to denote the geodesic
\pi_{0}
, which is defined through the same expression.

As already alluded, in our computations to obtain finite dimensional Gaussianity, it is more convenient to use another optimizer

\pi^{*}(s)=\argmax_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,s_{-};x,s)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s;0,s_{+}).

Here
s_{-}\in[0,s)
and
s_{+}\in(s,1]
.
In the rest of this section, all the constants can depend on
s_{-},s,s_{+}
.

The main goal of this section is the following closeness between
\pi(s)
and
\pi^{*}(s)
.
As in previous sections, we write
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)
.

Proposition 10.1
.

For any
L\geq 2
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(|\pi(s)-\pi^{*}(s)|>L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}).

The case where
s_{-}=0
and
s_{+}=1
is obvious, since then
\pi(s)=\pi^{*}(s)
.
Below we write the proof for the case where
s_{-}>0
and
s_{+}<1
.
The proof for the other two cases are similar and we omit them.

For the rest of this section, we shall take the shorthand
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{i-1},s_{i-1};x_{i},s_{i})
for some
\vec{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and
x_{0}=x_{4}=0
,
s_{0}=0
,
s_{1}=s_{-}
,
s_{2}=s
,
s_{3}=s_{+}
,
s_{4}=1
.
We also write
\smash{\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}}
for the vector
\{\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{4}
; in other words, we now have four parts instead of two like in previous sections.
For any vector
\vec{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
denotes the event where
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\in h_{i}+[0,e^{-L}]
for each
i
.

Our general idea is to replace the conditioning
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
by
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
, for some reasonable
\vec{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
and
\vec{h}
.
Then we bound
|\pi(s)-x_{2}|
conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
, using coalescence and the tent picture.

We first restrict
\pi^{*}
to an interval of length
<L^{1/2}
.

Lemma 10.2
.

For each
i=1,2,3
and
L
large,

\mathbb{P}\left(|\pi^{*}(s_{i})|>L^{5/16}\log L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}).

Proof.

We write the proof for
i=2
; the other two cases follow similarly.

Let
\mathcal{E}
denote the event where
|\pi^{*}(s)|>L^{5/16}\log L
.
By
Lemma

6.4
we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)>1-C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})
, where
\mathcal{E}_{1}
is the event

\inf_{|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|,|x_{3}|\leq L^{-1/4}\log L}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\geq L-L^{5/8}\log L.

We have that
\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\mathcal{E}_{2}
, where
\mathcal{E}_{2}
is the event

\sup_{|x_{2}|>L^{5/16}\log L,x_{1}=x_{3}=0}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\geq L-L^{5/8}\log L.

By the shear invariance property and
Corollary

6.2
, and a union bound, we have that

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2})<C\exp\left(-\frac{4}{3}L^{3/2}-cL^{9/8}(\log L)^{2}\right)
.
By combining this with the tail estimate
Theorem

2.13
we get that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)<C\exp(-cL^{9/8}(\log L)^{2})
.
As we can upper bound
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)
by
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}^{c}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)
, the conclusion follows.
∎

In replacing the conditioning
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
into
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
, we will need to bound the ratio

\mathbb{P}(\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)^{-1}
, for which we need that
\sum_{i=1}^{4}h_{i}>L-\log L
.
For this we will invoke
Proposition

9.2
, as well as a rough lower bound of
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}
conditional on the upper tail
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
.

Lemma 10.3
.

For any
\vec{x}\in[-L^{5/16}\log L,L^{5/16}\log L]^{3}
, and
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}<(s_{i}-s_{i-1})L-L^{27/32}\log L)\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}).

Proof.

We prove this for
i=2
, and the other cases would follow verbatim.

We use
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{j,0}
to denote
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{j}
with each of
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}
replaced by
0
.
Let
\mathcal{E}
denote the event
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}<(s_{2}-s_{1})L-L^{27/32}\log L
, and define
\mathcal{E}_{0}
by

\mathcal{E}_{0}=\left\{\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{j,0}>(s_{j}-s_{j-1})L-L^{53/64}\right\},

for each
j=1,2,3,4
.
By
Lemma

6.4
, we have

(10.1)

\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{4}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{j,0}<L-L^{5/8}\log L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}).

By
Lemma

6.3
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{c},\sum_{j=1}^{4}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{j,0}\geq L-L^{5/8}\log L\right)\\
<C\exp(-cL^{37/32})\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{4}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{j,0}\geq L-L^{5/8}\log L\right).

By
Theorem

2.13
and
Corollary

6.2
, we can bound the ratio of the last factor over
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)
by
C\exp(CL^{9/8}\log L)
.
Therefore we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{c},\sum_{j=1}^{4}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{j,0}\geq L-L^{5/8}\log L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-cL^{37/32}).

Combining this with (
10.1
) implies that

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{0}^{c}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})
.

We next upper bound
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{E}_{0})
.
In the case where
x_{1}x_{2}<0
, by the shift-invariance (
Lemma

3.3
)

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{E}_{0})=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,s_{1};x_{2}-x_{1},s_{2})<(s_{2}-s_{1})L-L^{27/32}\log L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}_{0}\right).

By
Lemma

2.20
and using that
|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|\leq L^{5/16}\log L
, this is bounded by
C\exp(-cL^{11/8}\log L)
.

In the case where
x_{1}x_{2}\geq 0
,
by
Lemma

2.20
and using that
|x_{1}|,|x_{2}|\leq L^{5/16}\log L
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,s_{1};x_{2},s_{2})<(s_{2}-s_{1})L-L^{27/32}(\log L)/2\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}_{0}\right)<C\exp(-cL^{11/8}\log L),

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{1},s_{1};0,s_{2})<(s_{2}-s_{1})L-L^{27/32}(\log L)/2\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}_{0}\right)<C\exp(-cL^{11/8}\log L).

Then by the quadrangle inequalities (
2.1
) and (
2.4
), we still have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{E}_{0})<C\exp(-cL^{11/8}\log L)
.
Then since (by
Theorem

2.13
)
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{0})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)^{-1}<C\exp(CL^{85/64})
,
we have

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)&<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{E}_{0}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)\\
&\leq C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\cap\mathcal{E}_{0})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)^{-1}\\
&\leq C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\mid\mathcal{E}_{0})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{0})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)^{-1}\\
&<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}).\end{split}

Thus the conclusion follows.
∎

We next prove that, given the tail
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}
,
\pi(s)
should be close to
x_{2}
.
Define

\mathrm{Val}^{4}=\Bigg\{\vec{h}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:h_{i}\geq(s_{i}-s_{i-1})L-L^{27/32}\log L,\;\forall i=1,2,3,4;\\
L-\log L\leq\sum_{i=1}^{4}h_{i}<L+(\log L)^{2}\Bigg\}.

Lemma 10.4
.

For any
\vec{x}\in[-L^{5/16}\log L,L^{5/16}\log L]^{3}
, and
\vec{h}\in\mathrm{Val}^{4}
,

\mathbb{P}\left(|\pi(s)-x_{2}|>L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}/2\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}\right)<C\exp(-cL^{1/2}(\log L)^{2}).

Proof.

By
Theorem

2.13
we have

(10.2)

\mathbb{P}(\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h})>c\exp\left(-\frac{4}{3}L^{3/2}-CL^{9/8}(\log L)^{2}\right).

Therefore, using
Lemma

6.1
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x_{2},s)<h_{1}+h_{2}-C\log L\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}\right)<C\exp(-cL^{2}),

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{2},s;0,1)<h_{3}+h_{4}-C\log L\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}\right)<C\exp(-cL^{2}).

Thus

(10.3)

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x_{2},s)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{2},s;0,1)<L-C\log L\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}\right)<C\exp(-cL^{2}).

We next restrict the intervals. We define
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{out}}
as follows. For
\beta=\infty
, we let

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{out}}=\sup_{\vec{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\setminus[-L^{3/8},L^{3/8}]^{3}}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}.

For
\beta=1
, we let

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{out}}=\left(\sup_{|x_{2}|>L^{3/8}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{2},s;0,1)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x_{2},s)\right)\vee\\
\left(\sup_{|x_{2}|\leq L^{3/8}}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{out},-}(x_{2})+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{2},s;0,1))\vee(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x_{2},s)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{out},+}(x_{2}))\right),

where

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{out},-}(x_{2})=\log\int_{|x_{1}|\geq L^{3/8}}\exp\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x_{1},

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{out},+}(x_{2})=\log\int_{|x_{3}|\geq L^{3/8}}\exp\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{3}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{4}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x_{3}.

Using
Corollary

6.2
and shear invariance, and a union bound, we can upper bound
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{out}}>L-(\log L)^{3/2})
by
C\exp\left(-\frac{4}{3}L^{3/2}-cL^{5/4}(\log L)^{2}\right)
.
Then by (
10.2
) we have

(10.4)

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{out}}>L-(\log L)^{3/2}\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}\right)<C\exp(-cL^{5/4}(\log L)^{2}).

We next consider the restricted part.

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{res}}(x_{2})=\max_{x_{1},x_{3}\in[-L^{3/8},L^{3/8}]}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i},

for
\beta=\infty
, and

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{res}}(x_{2})=\log\int_{|x_{1}|\leq L^{3/8}}\exp\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{1}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{2}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x_{1}+\log\int_{|x_{3}|\leq L^{3/8}}\exp\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{3}+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{4}\right)\,\mathrm{d}x_{3},

for
\beta=1
.
Using
Proposition

3.4
and
Corollary

2.21
, one can upper bound
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{res}}
assuming a coalescence event, as in the proof of
Proposition

9.1
. Omitting the details, we can deduce that

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{x\in I}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{res}}(x)>L-(\log L)^{3/2}\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}\right)<C\exp(-cL^{1/2}(\log L)^{2}),

where
I=[-L^{3/8},L^{3/8}]\setminus[x_{2}-L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}/2,x_{2}+L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}/2]
.
This with (
10.4
) implies that

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{|x-x_{2}|>L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}/2}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x_{2},s)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{2},s;0,1)>L-(\log L)^{3/2}\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}\right)\\
<C\exp(-cL^{1/2}(\log L)^{2}).

This and (
10.3
) imply the conclusion.
∎

We now finish proving
Proposition

10.1
, using all the above ingredients as well as
Proposition

9.2
, which lower bounds the sum
\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}
by
L-\log L
, given that
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
.

Proof of
Proposition

10.1
.

We can assume that
L
is large enough, since otherwise the conclusion follows trivially.

Denote
w_{\beta=1}=L^{-1/2}
and
w_{\beta=\infty}=L^{-1}
as in the previous section.
By Lemmas
10.2
and
10.3
, we can bound the LHS in the display by

(10.5)

\sum_{\vec{x}\in([-L^{5/16}\log L,L^{5/16}\log L]\cap w_{\beta}\mathbb{Z})^{3}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i=1,2,3}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta},|\pi(s)-x_{2}|>L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}/2\\
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\geq(s_{i}-s_{i-1})L-L^{27/32}\log L,\forall i=1,2,3,4\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\Big)+C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}).

By
Lemma

6.1
and
Theorem

4.1
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\geq L+(\log L)^{2}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-cL^{1/2}(\log L)^{2}).

This together with
Proposition

9.2
implies that, for any
\vec{x}\in([-L^{5/16}\log L,L^{5/16}\log L]\cap w_{\beta}\mathbb{Z})^{3}
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i=1,2,3}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta},\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\not\in\mathrm{Val}^{4},\\
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\geq(s_{i}-s_{i-1})L-L^{27/32}\log L,\forall i=1,2,3,4\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\Big)<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}).

Then each summand in (
10.5
) can be bounded by

\sum_{\vec{h}}\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{i=1,2,3}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}|\leq w_{\beta},|\pi(s)-x_{2}|>L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}/2,\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h},\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\Big)\\
+C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}),

where the sum is over
<\exp(CL)
many
\vec{h}\in\mathrm{Val}^{4}
, such that any element in
\mathrm{Val}^{4}
is
\approx\vec{h}
for one of them.
We note that the summand for each
\vec{h}
is bounded by

\mathbb{P}\left(|\pi(s)-x_{2}|>L^{-1/2}(\log L)^{2}/2\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h}\right)\mathbb{P}(\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\approx\vec{h})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)^{-1}.

By
Lemma

10.4
, the first factor is bounded by
C\exp(-cL^{1/2}(\log L)^{2})
.
Then the sum over
\vec{h}
is bounded by

C\exp(-cL^{1/2}(\log L)^{2})\mathbb{P}(\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\in\mathrm{Val}^{4})\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)^{-1}.

By
Lemma

6.1
and
Theorem

4.1
, we can bound this by
C\exp(CL^{1/2}\log L)
, since
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta}\in\mathrm{Val}^{4}
implies that
\sum_{i=1}^{4}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{i}\geq L-\log L
.
Now since each summand in (
10.5
) is bounded by
C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})
, plugging this estimate back leads to the conclusion.
∎

11.
Finite dimensional Brownian bridge limit

In this section, we prove finite dimensional convergence of
\pi_{0}
and
\Gamma_{0}
to Brownian bridge (under upper tails).
As in previous sections we take the setup of
(s_{1},\cdots,s_{k-1})\in\mathring{\Lambda}_{k-1}([0,1])
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and denote
s_{0}=0
and
s_{k}=1
; and all the constants within this section can depend on
k
and
(s_{1},\cdots,s_{k-1})
.
Also recall that we define

\pi^{*}(s_{i})=\argmax_{x}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,s_{i-1};x,s_{i})+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s_{i};0,s_{i+1}),

for each
i=1,\ldots,k-1
. We adopt the shorthand
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;0,1)
.

We shall prove that as
L\to\infty
,
L^{1/4}\{\pi^{*}(s_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k-1}
conditional on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
converges to a joint Gaussian, matching that for a Brownian bridge.
We note that even in zero temperature,
\pi^{*}(s_{i})
does not a priori coincide with
\pi_{0}(s_{i})=\pi(s_{i})
, which is instead given as the maximizer of processes from height
0
to
s_{i}
and
s_{i}
to
1
(instead of
s_{i-1}
to
s_{i}
and
s_{i}
to
s_{i+1}
here), i.e.,
\pi(s_{i})=\argmax_{z}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;z,s_{i})+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(z,s_{i};0,1)
.
However, we have shown that, conditional on the upper tail, with high probability
|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-\pi(s_{i})|
is of order smaller than
L^{-1/4}
(
Proposition

10.1
); and in positive temperature the polymer measure concentrates at height
s_{i}
in a window of order smaller than
L^{-1/4}
around
\pi(s_{i})
(
Proposition

7.1
).
Therefore, the Gaussian limit of
\{\pi^{*}(s_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k-1}
as follows suffices for us to deduce our main results.

We denote
w_{\beta=1}=L^{-1/2}
and
w_{\beta=\infty}=L^{-1}
as before.
Fix any compact set
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k-1}
, and all the constants below can depend on
\mathcal{K}
.

Theorem 11.1
.

As
L\to\infty
, uniformly over
\vec{x},\vec{y}\in\mathcal{K}
(with
x_{0}=x_{k}=y_{0}=y_{k}=0
for the convenience of notations),

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq w_{\beta}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-y_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq w_{\beta}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)}

\displaystyle\to\exp\left(-2\left[\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{(x_{i}-x_{i-1})^{2}-(y_{i}-y_{i-1})^{2}}{s_{i}-s_{i-1}}\right]\right).

We note that the RHS is the ratio of the joint density of
(\frac{1}{2}B(s_{1}),\ldots,\frac{1}{2}B(s_{k-1}))
evaluated at
\vec{x}
and
\vec{y}
, where
B
is a standard Brownian bridge on
[0,1]
.

Note that this is a comparison of probabilities, while we wish to show weak convergence. The following lemma allows the transition. Its proof is fairly straightforward real analysis and we relegate it to Appendix
A
.

Lemma 11.2
.

Let
d\geq 1
and suppose
\{\vec{X}_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon>0}
is a family of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued random vectors such that as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
\vec{X}_{\varepsilon}\to\vec{X}
in distribution for some random vector
\vec{X}
. Suppose also that there is a continuous strictly positive integrable function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to(0,\infty)
such that, for every compact set
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, uniformly over
\vec{x},\vec{y}\in\mathcal{K}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

(11.1)

\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\vec{X}_{\varepsilon}\in\vec{x}+[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)^{d}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\vec{X}_{\varepsilon}\in\vec{y}+[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)^{d}\right)}\cdot\frac{f(\vec{y})}{f(\vec{x})}\to 1.

Then
\vec{X}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and has density given by
f(\vec{x})/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\vec{z})\,\mathrm{d}\vec{z}
.

With these results in hand, we may prove our main theorems.

Proofs of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
.

The tightness of
\{L^{1/4}\pi_{0}\}_{L\geq 2}
and
\{L^{1/4}\Gamma_{0}\}_{L\geq 2}
conditional on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L
in the space
\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R})
is given by
Proposition

5.1
, and it remains to establish finite dimensional convergence.

For
B
being a standard Brownian bridge on
[0,1]
,

Theorem

11.1
and
Lemma

11.2
imply that
\{2L^{1/4}\pi^{*}(s_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k-1}\to\{B(s_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k-1}
in distribution.
Proposition

10.1
along with the Borel-Cantelli lemma guarantees that
L^{1/4}\pi(s_{i})-L^{1/4}\pi^{*}(s_{i})\to 0
almost surely for every
i
. This implies that, for
\beta=\infty
,
\{2L^{1/4}\pi_{0}(s_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k-1}\to\{B(s_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k-1}
in distribution,
completing the proof of
Theorem

1.1
.

For
\beta=1
, taking expectations in
Proposition

7.1
further yields that
\mathbb{P}(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\Gamma_{0}(s_{i})-\pi^{*}(s_{i})|\leq ML^{-1/2}\log L\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)>1-L^{-cM}-C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})
, for
M
being a large enough constant. Thus
L^{1/4}\Gamma_{0}(s_{i})-L^{1/4}\pi^{*}(s_{i})\to 0
almost surely for every
i
. We then obtain that
\{2L^{1/4}\Gamma_{0}(s_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k-1}\to\{B(s_{i})\}_{i=1}^{k-1}
in distribution,
completing the proof of
Theorem

1.2
.
∎

The rest of this section and the next are devoted to proving
Theorem

11.1
.

11.1.
Finite dimensional convergence

We introduce some useful shorthand to make the notation simpler:
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}
is a vector whose
i
th
component is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{i-1}L^{-1/4},s_{i-1};x_{i}L^{-1/4},s_{i})

Below we also use
o(1)
to denote any quantity that
\to 0
as
L\to\infty
.

Proof of
Theorem

11.1
.

We start by noting that,
by
Lemma

6.4
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}<L-L^{5/8}\log L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2});

and by
Lemma

6.1
, plus
Theorem

2.13
and
Theorem

4.1
, for some
C_{0}>0

\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}>L+C_{0}\log L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)<C\exp(-cL^{1/2}\log L).

So we can upper bound
\mathbb{P}(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq w_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)
by

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sum}},\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|<w_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)+C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})\cdot\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L),

where
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sum}}
is the event

L-L^{5/8}\log L\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\leq L+C_{0}\log L.

Take
M_{*}
to be a large constant, and let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{prop}}(M_{*})
be defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{prop}}(M_{*})=\bigcap_{i=1}^{k}\left\{\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\geq(s_{i}-s_{i-1})L-M_{*}L^{7/8}\right\}.

Using
Lemma

6.3
(proportionality statement), and
Corollary

6.2
and
Theorem

2.13
(tail bounds for the sum
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{prop}}^{c},\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\geq L-L^{5/8}\log L\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-cM_{*}^{2}L^{5/4}\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\geq L-L^{5/8}\log L\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-cM_{*}^{2}L^{5/4}-\frac{4}{3}(L-L^{5/8}\log L)^{3/2}+CL^{3/4}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-cM_{*}^{2}L^{5/4}+CL^{9/8}\log L\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right).

As
M_{*}
is large enough, this is upper bounded by
\exp(-cM_{*}^{2}L^{5/4})\cdot\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)
.

Thus far, we have overall shown that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|<w_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{prop}}(M_{*}),\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sum}},\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|<w_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)+C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})\cdot\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L).

Set (as before)
r_{\beta=1}=1
and
r_{\beta=\infty}=L^{-1/2}
.
Using
Proposition

9.2
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|<w_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)

(11.2)

\displaystyle\leq\bigl(1-e^{-cM_{*}^{2}L^{1/2}r_{\beta}}\bigr)^{-1}\mathbb{P}\left(\parbox{296.30743pt}{\centering$\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{prop}}(M_{*}),\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sum}},\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|<w_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L$\\
$\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}>L+(k-1)\beta^{-1}\log(2L^{1/2})-M_{*}r_{\beta}$\@add@centering}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right).

We next do a restriction. Recall the notation
\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\vec{x}\in I}\kern 3.02678pt}{\emaxop_{\vec{x}\in I}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{x}\in I}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{x}\in I}^{(\beta)}}}f
for
f:\mathbb{R}^{k-1}\to\mathbb{R}
and a set
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k-1}
from (
9.1
).
Define

(11.3)

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]=\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\|\vec{z}\|_{\infty}\leq 1}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{\|\vec{z}\|_{\infty}\leq 1}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\|\vec{z}\|_{\infty}\leq 1}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\|\vec{z}\|_{\infty}\leq 1}^{(\beta)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(s_{i-1},z_{i-1};s_{i},z_{i}),

where
z_{0}=z_{k}=0
.
Then, by the (quenched) one-point transversal fluctuation estimate (
Proposition

8.4
for
\beta=1
and
Lemma

5.2
for
\beta=\infty
), (
11.2
) is bounded by

(11.4)

\begin{split}&\bigl(1-e^{-cM_{*}^{2}L^{1/2}r_{\beta}}\bigr)^{-1}\mathbb{P}\left(\parbox{263.78505pt}{\centering$\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{prop}}(M_{*}),\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sum}},\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|<w_{\beta}$,\\
$\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>(1-\beta^{-1}e^{-cL^{1/4}})L$,\\
$\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}>L+(k-1)\beta^{-1}\log(2L^{1/2})-M_{*}r_{\beta}$\@add@centering}\right)\\
&\qquad+C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right).\end{split}

To analyze the first term in the previous display, we define

(11.5)

E_{\mathrm{val}}=\left\{(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\in(e^{-L}\mathbb{Z})^{k}:\parbox{312.9803pt}{\centering$L+(k-1)\beta^{-1}\log(2L^{1/2})-(M_{*}+1)r_{\beta}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}\leq L+C_{0}\log L$, \\
$h_{i}\in(s_{i}-s_{i-1})L+[-2M_{*}L^{7/8},kM_{*}L^{7/8}]$ for $i=1,\ldots,k$\@add@centering}\right\}.

Here we take the fine mesh
(e^{-L}\mathbb{Z})^{k}
instead of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
because (as mentioned before) we will need to apply
Proposition

3.4
and
Proposition

3.5
. We also recall the notation
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
for the event that each
\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\in h_{i}+[0,e^{-L}]
.

Now we see that the probability in the first term of (
11.4
) is bounded by

(11.6)

\sum_{\vec{h}\in E_{\mathrm{val}}}\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|<w_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>(1-\beta^{-1}e^{-cL^{1/4}})L\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}\Bigr)\cdot\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}\Bigr).

Our goal is to relate each of these terms to the corresponding one with
\vec{x}
replaced by
\vec{y}
, which we state precisely in the next two lemmas to be proved later. The first factor is essentially unchanged:

Lemma 11.3
.

For
\vec{h}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\in E_{\mathrm{val}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|<w_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>(1-\beta^{-1}e^{-cL^{1/4}})L\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq(1+o(1))\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-y_{i}L^{-1/4}|<w_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{y}}\approx\vec{h}\Bigr).

The proof of this lemma is somewhat involved and will be given in
Section

12
.
For the second factor in (
11.6
), we have the following statement, which is the source of the Brownian bridge density in our result. Its proof is a straightforward consequence of
Theorem

4.1
, and we give it after completing the proof of
Theorem

11.1
.

Lemma 11.4
.

For
(h_{1},\ldots,h_{k})\in E_{\mathrm{val}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}\right)

\displaystyle=(1+o(1))\exp\left(-2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{(x_{i}-x_{i-1})^{2}-(y_{i}-y_{i-1})^{2}}{s_{i}-s_{i-1}}\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{y}}\approx\vec{h}\right).

Inputting the information from the previous two lemmas into (
11.6
) and (
11.4
) yields that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq w_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)

\displaystyle<C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)

\displaystyle\quad+(1+o(1))\left(1+\exp(-cM_{*}^{2}L^{1/2}r_{\beta})\right)\exp\left(-2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{(x_{i}-x_{i-1})^{2}-(y_{i}-y_{i-1})^{2})}{s_{i}-s_{i-1}}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{\vec{h}\in E_{\mathrm{val}}}\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-y_{i}L^{-1/4}|<w_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{y}}\approx\vec{h}\Bigr)\cdot\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{y}}\approx\vec{h}).

Therefore we have, since
\{\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{y}}\approx\vec{h}\}
are disjoint events for distinct
\vec{h}\in E_{\mathrm{val}}
,

(11.7)

\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq w_{\beta}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)\\
&\leq(1+o(1))\left(1+e^{-cM_{*}^{2}L^{1/2}r_{\beta}}\right)\exp\left(-2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{(x_{i}-x_{i-1})^{2}-(y_{i}-y_{i-1})^{2})}{s_{i}-s_{i-1}}\right)\\
&\qquad\times\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-y_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq w_{\beta}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)+C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}).\end{split}

We need to lower bound
\mathbb{P}(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-y_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq w_{\beta}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)
in order to ensure that the error term
C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})
is not dominating. For this we sum the previous display over
\vec{x}\in[-KL^{-1/4},KL^{-1/4}]^{k}\cap(w_{\beta}Z)^{k}
, where
K
is a large constant such that

\sum_{\vec{x}\in[-KL^{-1/4},KL^{-1/4}]^{k}\cap(w_{\beta}Z)^{k}}\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq w_{\beta}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)\geq\frac{1}{2};

that this is possible follows from combining transversal fluctuation bounds for
\pi(s_{i})
(
Proposition

7.1
and
Proposition

7.3
for
\beta=1
or
Lemma

5.2
for
\beta=\infty
) and the bound on the closeness of
\pi(s_{i})
and
\pi^{*}(s_{i})
(Proposition
10.1
). Then from (
11.7
) we have that

\displaystyle\left(\tfrac{1}{2}-C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2})\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-y_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq w_{\beta}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\sum_{\vec{x}\in[-KL^{-1/4},KL^{-1/4}]^{k}\cap(w_{\beta}Z)^{k}}\exp\left(-2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{(x_{i}-x_{i-1})^{2}-(y_{i}-y_{i-1})^{2})}{s_{i}-s_{i-1}}\right).

This yields that
\mathbb{P}(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-y_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq w_{\beta}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L)
is lower bounded by a polynomial in
L^{-1}
.
Thus we conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq w_{\beta}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-y_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq w_{\beta}\mid\mathcal{L}^{\beta}>L\right)}

\displaystyle\leq(1+o(1))\left(1+e^{-cM_{*}^{2}L^{1/2}r_{\beta}}\right)\exp\left(-2\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{(x_{i}-x_{i-1})^{2}-(y_{i}-y_{i-1})^{2})}{s_{i}-s_{i-1}}\right)

\displaystyle+C\exp(-c(\log L)^{2}).

We can then get a lower bound of the same ratio by swapping
\vec{x}
and
\vec{y}
.
Taking
L\to\infty
followed by
M_{*}\to\infty
completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
11.4
.

It suffices to prove the case
\vec{y}=\vec{0}
and apply the resulting statement twice. By independence,
\mathbb{P}\left(\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\in h_{i}+[0,e^{-L}]\right)
. So we have to show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\in h_{i}+[0,e^{-L}]\right)=(1+o(1))\exp\left(-\frac{2x_{i}^{2}}{s_{i}-s_{i-1}}\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{0}}_{i}\in h_{i}+[0,e^{-L}]\right).

Now, by shear invariance and using Theorem
4.1
in the third line,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\in h_{i}+[0,e^{-L}]\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\geq h_{i}\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\geq h_{i}+e^{-L}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{0}}_{i}\geq h_{i}+\frac{(x_{i}-x_{i-1})^{2}L^{-1/2}}{s_{i}-s_{i-1}}\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{0}}_{i}\geq h_{i}+e^{-L}+\frac{(x_{i}-x_{i-1})^{2}L^{-1/2}}{s_{i}-s_{i-1}}\right)

\displaystyle=(1+o(1))\exp\left(-2(s_{i}-s_{i-1})^{-3/2}h_{i}^{1/2}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})^{2}L^{-1/2}\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{0}}_{i}\geq h_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\quad-(1+o(1))\exp\left(-2(s_{i}-s_{i-1})^{-3/2}(h_{i}+e^{-L})^{1/2}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})^{2}L^{-1/2}\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{0}}_{i}\geq h_{i}+e^{-L}\right).

Since
(h_{i}+e^{-L})^{1/2}=h_{i}^{1/2}+O(e^{-L})
, and
h_{i}^{1/2}=(s_{i}-s_{i-1})^{1/2}L^{1/2}+o(L^{1/2})
due to that
h_{i}=(s_{i}-s_{i-1})L+o(L)
, the previous display equals

\displaystyle(1+o(1))\exp\left(-\frac{2(x_{i}-x_{i-1})^{2}}{s_{i}-s_{i-1}}\right)\left[\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{0}}_{i}\geq h_{i}\right)-\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{0}}_{i}\geq h_{i}+e^{-L}\right)\right],

which is what we wanted to show.
∎

12.
Joint comparison of maximizer location and free energy across peaks

In this section, we give the proof of Lemma
11.3
.
We use the setup there: in particular,
C_{0},M_{*}
are large constants,
L
is taken to be large (depending on
C_{0},M_{*}
),
\vec{h}\in E_{\mathrm{val}}
for
E_{\mathrm{val}}
defined in (
11.5
),

\vec{x},\vec{y}\in\mathcal{K}
for a compact set
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{k-1}
, and

(s_{1},\cdots,s_{k-1})\in\mathring{\Lambda}_{k-1}([0,1])
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, with
s_{0}=0
and
s_{k}=1
.
All the constants within this section can depend on
C_{0}
,
M_{*}
,
\mathcal{K}
,
k
, and
(s_{1},\cdots,s_{k-1})
.

The proof strategy is to do a resampling on a small interval
I
(with size of order
L^{-1/2}\log L
) in a way that a certain conditional probability of the event in question (the location of
\pi^{*}(s_{i})
for
i=1,\ldots,k
and a lower bound on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]
) is a function of the endpoint values at the boundary of
I
. The proof then comes down to showing that the density of these endpoint values under the conditioning
\smash{\mathcal{L}^{\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}}
(which recall is shorthand for
\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\in h_{i}+[0,e^{-L}]
for
i=1,\ldots,k
) is
1+o(1)
of the density of the same under the conditioning
\mathcal{L}^{\vec{y}}\approx\vec{h}
.

However, note that neither of the events
\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|<w_{\beta}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>(1-\beta^{-1}\exp(-cL^{1/4}))L
are functions of the profile on an interval of size of order
L^{-1/2}\log L
. Thus we will need to first argue that we can consider different events which do have this localized property. For the first event, we simply consider the maximizer on
I
as a proxy, instead of on
\mathbb{R}
; clearly, if the first event holds, then it also holds that the restricted maximizer is
w_{\beta}
-close to
x_{i}L^{-1/4}
. We will then show (Lemma
12.7
) that with high probability the restricted maximizer and true maximizer coincide. In the
\beta=\infty
case, on this event we also have that
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]
is a function of the profile on an interval of size of order
L^{-1/2}\log L
.

Modifying the second event,
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>(1-\exp(-cL^{1/4}))L
, to be a local function of the profile in the
\beta=1
case is more difficult. A naive argument (invoking
Proposition

7.1
and
Proposition

10.1
) shows that the free energy when restricted to an interval of size
ML^{-1/2}\log L
(for a large number
M
) would capture a
(1-L^{-M})
fraction of the total free energy. But at the end of the comparison we need to be able to return to the event of being larger than
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>L
, and so we need to argue that, conditional on
\mathcal{L}^{\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>(1-\exp(-cL^{1/4}))L
(as well as the location of the restricted maximizer), with high probability
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}
will actually be larger than
L+L^{-E_{*}}
for some
E_{*}
, i.e., the free energy overshoots by a polynomial amount. Then we know that restricting to a
ML^{-1/2}\log L
window still results in the free energy being larger than
L
(by picking
M
large enough), and we can do our localized comparison argument with this event.

In the following
Section

12.1
we give the argument for saying that, conditional on
\mathcal{L}^{\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>(1-\exp(-cL^{1/4}))L
, it holds with high probability that
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>L+L^{-E_{*}}
(Lemma
12.1
). In Section
12.2
we argue that the free energy (in a form suitable for analysis in the upcoming proof of Lemma
11.3
) from the smaller interval of size
ML^{-1/2}\log L
is also
L+L^{-E_{*}}
(Lemma
12.5
). Then in Section
12.3
we give the proof of Lemma
11.3
.

Denote
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}
to be the event where
\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})|<w_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>(1-\beta^{-1}e^{-cL^{1/4}})L,\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
.
The following will be frequently used, to relate the conditioning on
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}
and
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
for
\vec{h}\in E_{\mathrm{val}}
with
E_{\mathrm{val}}
as defined in (
11.5
):

(12.1)

\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})|<w_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>(1-\beta^{-1}e^{-cL^{1/4}})L\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}\Bigg)>c\exp(-CL^{1/2}).

It follows from the lower bound in
Proposition

9.1
and
\vec{h}\in E_{\mathrm{val}}
.

12.1.
Conditional overshoot at positive temperature

We work in the case of
\beta=1
only in this subsection, in which case we recall that
w_{\beta}=L^{-1/2}
.

Lemma 12.1
.

There exists a constant
\rho>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]<L+\rho L^{-3}\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}\Big)<CL^{-1}.

This lemma is proved by a resampling argument, and we explain the setup next.
For a function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and an interval
[a,b]
, we define the
bridge of
f
on
[a,b]
, denoted
f^{[a,b]}
, by

\displaystyle f^{[a,b]}(x)=f(x)-\frac{x-a}{b-a}f(b)-\frac{b-x}{b-a}f(a);

in words, it is the function obtained by affinely shifting
f
to equal
0
at
a
and
b
.

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{+}[1](x)=\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{|z_{2}|,\ldots,|z_{k-1}|\leq 1}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{|z_{2}|,\ldots,|z_{k-1}|\leq 1}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{|z_{2}|,\ldots,|z_{k-1}|\leq 1}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{|z_{2}|,\ldots,|z_{k-1}|\leq 1}^{(\beta)}}}\sum_{i=2}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(z_{i-1},s_{i-1};z_{i},s_{i}),

with
z_{1}=x
,
z_{k}=0
; note that this is a function of
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}
on the temporal strip
[s_{1},1]
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},2}
be the top two lines in the line ensemble associated with
\smash{\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;\cdot,s_{1})}
; in particular
\smash{\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;\cdot,s_{1})}
. Let
\mathcal{F}
be the
\sigma
-algebra generated by

\bullet

\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(z)
for
z\in(-\infty,x_{1}L^{-1/4}]\cup[x_{1}L^{-1/4}+w_{\beta},\infty)
,

\bullet

(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1})^{x_{1}L^{-1/4}+[0,\frac{1}{2}w_{\beta}]}
,
(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1})^{x_{1}L^{-1/4}+[\frac{1}{2}w_{\beta},w_{\beta}]}
,

\bullet

\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},2}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{+}[1]
.

In particular, conditional on
\mathcal{F}
, the remaining randomness (to determine
\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}
) is the value of
U:=\smash{\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{1}{2}w_{\beta},s_{1})}=\smash{\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{1}{2}w_{\beta})}
, by linear interpolation.
Therefore, for
z\in[x_{1}L^{-1/4},x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{1}{2}w_{\beta}]
and
u\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote

(12.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{\beta,u}_{s_{1},1}(z):=\frac{z-x_{1}L^{-1/4}}{\frac{1}{2}w_{\beta}}\bigl(u-\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x_{1}L^{-1/4})\bigr)+\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x_{1}L^{-1/4})+(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1})^{x_{1}L^{-1/4}+[0,\frac{1}{2}w_{\beta}]}(z)

and a similar expression for
z\in\smash{[x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{1}{2}w_{\beta},x_{1}L^{-1/4}+w_{\beta}]}
.
Further, given
\mathcal{F}
and
U
, we also determine the value of
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]
via the formula for
\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}
as well as the convolution formula, since
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{+}[1]
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable. We also observe that the dependence of
\smash{\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]}
on
U
is increasing since the convolution formula has an increasing dependence on
\smash{\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}}
, which itself depends on
U
in an increasing manner.

Now we apply the Brownian Gibbs property. It implies that the distribution of
U
is a normal random variable of
\mathcal{F}
-measurable mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
given by

(12.3)

\begin{split}\mu&=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x_{1}L^{-1/4}+w_{\beta})+\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x_{1}L^{-1/4})\right)\\
\sigma^{2}&=2\cdot\frac{\frac{1}{2}w_{\beta}\cdot\frac{1}{2}w_{\beta}}{w_{\beta}}=\tfrac{1}{2}w_{\beta},\end{split}

tilted by the Radon-Nikodym derivative
W^{\mathrm{pt}}(U)/Z^{\mathrm{pt}}
, where
W^{\mathrm{pt}}
and
Z^{\mathrm{pt}}
are given by

W^{\mathrm{pt}}(u)=W(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta,u}_{s_{1},1},\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},2}),\quad Z^{\mathrm{pt}}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{F}}\left[W^{\mathrm{pt}}(U)\right].

where
W(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta,u}_{s_{1},1},\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},2})
is from (
2.6
) for the interval
[x_{1}L^{-1/4},x_{1}L^{-1/4}+w_{\beta}]
.

Now, if we require that
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>(1-e^{-cL^{1/4}})L
, this is equivalent to
U
being larger than some
\mathcal{F}
-determined value, due to the increasing dependence of
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]
on
U
already noted.
Further, if we also require that
|\pi^{*}(s_{1})|\leq w_{\beta}
, it is not hard to see that this also is equivalent to a lower bound on
U
(which may be
-\infty
).

These in turn imply that, conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}
, and
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
, the distribution of
U
is given by a Gaussian random variable of mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
as given in (
12.3
) tilted by
W^{\mathrm{pt}}(U)/Z^{\mathrm{pt}}
, and is further conditioned to be larger than an
\mathcal{F}
-measurable random variable, which we denote by
\mathrm{Cor}
(short for “corner”, which is terminology introduced for an analogous object in
[
Ham16
]
). Now, since
W^{\mathrm{pt}}
is an increasing function, this Gaussian random variable stochastically dominates the Gaussian random variable
X
with the same mean and variance which is conditioned only to be larger than
\mathrm{Cor}
.

If we know that
\mathrm{Cor}
is not too high (say less than order
L
) and
\mu
is not too low (say
-\mu
less than order
L
), then the Gaussian random variable will overshoot by an amount at least polynomial in
L^{-1}
. This upper bound on
\mathrm{Cor}
and lower bound on
\mu
are recorded in the next two lemmas and will be proved shortly.

Lemma 12.2
.

There exists
K_{0}
such that for
K>K_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{Cor}>h_{1}+K\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}\right)\leq\exp(-cKL^{1/2}).

Lemma 12.3
.

There exists
K_{0}
such that,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mu<-K_{0}L\ \Big|\ \mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}\right)\leq\exp(-cL^{3/2}).

However, knowing that the Gaussian overshoots by a polynomial-in-
L^{-1}
amount does not immediately imply that the free energy overshoots
L
by a comparable amount. For this we need to additionally know that the contribution to the free energy from the interval that gets perturbed by resampling
U
is non-trivial (in fact, it contributes a positive proportion of the free energy, as we will show). This is recorded in the next lemma.

Lemma 12.4
.

There exists
\rho>0
such that, conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}
, with probability at least
1-\exp(-cL^{1/2})
,

\displaystyle\int_{x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{1}{4}w_{\beta}}^{x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{3}{4}w_{\beta}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{+}[1](x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x\geq\rho\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1].

With the previous three lemmas in hand, we now give the proof of Lemma
12.1
. Afterward, we will give the proofs of those lemmas.

Proof of Lemma
12.1
.

For every
u\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
\mathcal{L}^{\beta,u}[1]
the same way as
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]
through (
11.3
), except for replacing
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,0;s_{1},\cdot)=\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}
by
\mathfrak{h}^{\beta,u}_{s_{1},1}
.
We observe that, for any
\delta>0
,

\displaystyle\exp\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,U-\delta}[1]\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{[-1,1]\setminus[x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{1}{4}w_{\beta},x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{3}{4}w_{\beta}]}\exp\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta,U-\delta}_{1}(x)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{+}[1](x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{1}{4}w_{\beta}}^{x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{3}{4}w_{\beta}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta,U-\delta}_{1}(x)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{+}[1](x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Notice that
\mathfrak{h}_{s_{1},1}^{\beta,U-\delta}(x)\leq\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x)-\frac{1}{2}\delta
on
x_{1}L^{-1/4}+[\frac{1}{4}w_{\beta},\frac{3}{4}w_{\beta}]
(by the formula (
12.2
)). So the second term in the previous display is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\int_{x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{1}{4}w_{\beta}}^{x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{3}{4}w_{\beta}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{+}[1](x)-\tfrac{1}{2}\delta\right)\,\mathrm{d}x.

Note also that
\mathfrak{h}_{s_{1},1}^{\beta,U-\delta}(x)\leq\mathfrak{h}_{s_{1},1}^{\beta,U}(x)=\mathfrak{h}_{s_{1},1}^{\beta}(x)
, which gives an upper bound on the first term two displays above.
Then by
Lemma

12.4
, there exists
\rho>0
such that, with conditional probability at least
1-\exp(-cL^{1/2})
,
\exp(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,U-\delta}[1])
is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\left[1-\rho+e^{-\frac{1}{2}\delta}\rho\right]\int_{[-1,1]}\exp\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{+}[1](x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x=\left[1-\rho+e^{-\frac{1}{2}\delta}\rho\right]\exp(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]).

Since
e^{-x}\leq 1-x/2
for
x\in[0,1]
, we see that the square bracket factor is at most
1-\rho\delta/4
.
In summary, with conditional probability at least
1-\exp(-cL^{1/2})
, for any
\delta>0
sufficiently small,

(12.4)

\displaystyle\exp\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,U-\delta}[1]\right)\leq(1-\rho\delta/4)\exp\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]\right).

Recall from the discussion preceding Lemma
12.2
that, conditionally on
\mathcal{F}
,
U
stochastically dominates a random variable
X
which is Gaussian with mean
\mu
and variance
\sigma^{2}
as given in (
12.3
) and conditioned to stay above
\mathrm{Cor}
(i.e.,
X
is not tilted by
W^{\mathrm{pt}}(U)/Z^{\mathrm{pt}}
). Now, since (by
Lemma

12.2
and
Lemma

12.3
) with conditional probability at least
1-\exp(-cL^{1/2})
it holds that
\mathrm{Cor}\leq h_{1}+K_{0}
(so that in particular
\mathrm{Cor}=O(L)
) and
\mu\geq-K_{0}L
, it follows that, with conditional probability at least
1-L^{-1}
that

\displaystyle X\geq\mathrm{Cor}+L^{-3},

using standard estimates for the normal tail bound (
Lemma

2.22
).

In particular, since
U
stochastically dominates
X
, taking
\delta=L^{-3}
implies that
U-\delta\geq\mathrm{Cor}
. Since
\mathrm{Cor}
is such that for any
u\geq\mathrm{Cor}
it holds that
\mathcal{L}^{\beta,u}[1]\geq(1-e^{-cL^{1/4}})L
, we obtain from combining these observations with (
12.4
) that

\displaystyle\exp(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1])\geq(1+\tfrac{1}{5}\rho L^{-3})\exp(L).

Taking logarithms and relabeling
\rho
completes the proof.
∎

We next prove the three lemmas that have been assumed.

Proof of Lemma
12.2
.

We observe that by the definition of
\mathrm{Cor}
, it holds almost surely that
\mathrm{Cor}\leq\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{1}{2}w_{\beta})
. So we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{Cor}>h_{1}+K\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\tfrac{1}{2}w_{\beta})>h_{1}+K\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}\right).

By
Theorem

2.17
this is upper bounded by
\exp(-cKL^{1/2})
.
Combining this with (
12.1
) and taking
K
large enough, we obtain the claimed bound.
∎

Proof of Lemma
12.3
.

Since
\mu=\frac{1}{2}(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x_{1}L^{-1/4}+w_{\beta})+\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x_{1}L^{-1/4}))
, it holds by
Theorem

2.17
and the independence of
\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}
across
i
that
\mathbb{P}\left(\mu<-K_{0}L\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}\right)<\exp(-cL^{3/2})
. Combining this with (
12.1
) completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
12.4
.

Take
C_{*}
to be a large constant.
By using
Proposition

9.1
for a mesh of
L^{2}
many
x\in[-1,1]
, taking a union bound, and applying the unconditional local fluctuations estimates
Proposition

2.16
, we have

(12.5)

\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\max_{|x|\leq 1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s_{1};0,1)>\sum_{i=2}^{k}h_{i}-(k-2)\log(2L^{1/2})+\frac{1}{2}C_{*}\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}\Bigg)<\exp(-cC_{*}L^{1/2}).

Further by
Corollary

2.21
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x)\leq h_{1}-C_{*}-L^{1/2}|x-x_{1}L^{-1/4}|/2,\;\forall x:|x-x_{1}L^{-1/4}|\geq 10C_{*}w_{\beta},|x|\leq 1\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}\right)\\
>1-C\exp(-cC_{*}L^{1/2}).

We can replace the conditioning
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
in the above two estimates by
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}
, using (
12.1
).
Then from these, we have that with probability
>1-C\exp(-cL^{1/2})
conditioning on
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}
,

\displaystyle\int_{|x-x_{1}L^{-1/4}|\geq 10C_{*}w_{\beta},|x|\leq 1}\exp\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s_{1};0,1)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}-(k-2)\log(2L^{1/2})-\log(L^{1/2})-\tfrac{1}{2}C_{*}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(L-\tfrac{1}{4}C_{*})\leq\exp(-\tfrac{1}{4}C_{*})\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1],

using that
\vec{h}\in E_{\mathrm{val}}
and
C_{*}
is large for the second inequality, and that we have conditioned on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>L
for the last inequality. This implies that

(12.6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]\leq(1-\exp(-\tfrac{1}{4}C_{*}))^{-1}\int_{|x-x_{1}L^{-1/4}|\leq 10C_{*}w_{\beta}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{+}[1](x)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x.

It also follows from
Corollary

2.21
that, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cC_{*}L^{1/2})
conditioning on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
,

h_{1}-20C_{*}\leq\inf_{|x-x_{1}L^{-1/4}|\leq 10C_{*}w_{\beta}}\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x)\leq\sup_{|x-x_{1}L^{-1/4}|\leq 10C_{*}w_{\beta}}\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x)\leq h_{1}+C_{*}.

And by
Corollary

2.21
and
Proposition

3.4
, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cC_{*}L^{1/2})
conditioning on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
,
there is

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s_{i-1};y,s_{i})>h_{i}-30C_{*},

for any
i=2,\ldots,k
, and
|x-x_{i-1}L^{-1/4}|,|y-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq 10C_{*}w_{\beta}
; thus

\inf_{|x-x_{1}L^{-1/4}|\leq 10C_{*}w_{\beta}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{+}[1](x)\geq\sum_{i=2}^{k}h_{i}-(k-2)\log(2L^{1/2})-30kC_{*}.

Combining these two estimates with (
12.5
), we get that, with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cC_{*}L^{1/2})
conditioning on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\int_{x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{1}{4}w_{\beta}}^{x_{1}L^{-1/4}+\frac{3}{4}w_{\beta}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s_{1};0,1)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x}{\int_{x_{1}L^{-1/4}-10C_{*}w_{\beta}}^{x_{1}L^{-1/4}+10C_{*}w_{\beta}}\exp\left(\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{s_{1},1}(x)+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x,s_{1};0,1)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x}\geq(40C_{*})^{-1}\exp(-40kC_{*}).

We can further replace the conditioning by
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}
, using (
12.1
).
Then together with (
12.6
) the proof completes.
∎

12.2.
Free energy of restricted interval

In this subsection, we prove the following statement.

We introduce some notation to describe the free energy profiles in disjoint temporal strips defined by
[s_{i-1},s_{i}]
.
We define

(12.7)

\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{x}}_{1}=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{i-1}L^{-1/4},s_{i-1};x_{i}L^{-1/4}+\cdot,s_{i}),\quad\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{bot},i,\vec{x}}_{1}=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{i-1}L^{-1/4}+\cdot,s_{i-1};x_{i}L^{-1/4},s_{i}),

for each
i=1,\ldots,k
.
For any vector
\vec{z}\in\mathbb{R}^{k-1}
, we always write
z_{0}=z_{k}=0
for the convenience of notations.
We denote

(12.8)

\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{sum}}(\vec{z})=\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{x}}_{1}(z_{i})+\sum_{i=2}^{k}\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{bot},i,\vec{x}}_{1}(z_{i-1}),

We also write
I_{M,L}=[-ML^{-1/2}\log L,ML^{-1/2}\log L]
,
and
I_{\vec{x}}=L^{-1/4}\vec{x}+I_{M,L}^{k-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k-1}
.
Recall the
\emaxop\nolimits^{(\beta)}
notation from (
9.1
).

Lemma 12.5
.

When
\beta=1
, there exists
\rho>0
such that, conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}
, it holds with probability at least
1-CL^{-1}
that

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\vec{z}\in I^{k-1}_{M,L}}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I^{k-1}_{M,L}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I^{k-1}_{M,L}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I^{k-1}_{M,L}}^{(\beta)}}}\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{sum}}(\vec{z})\geq L+\rho L^{-3}+\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}.

When
\beta=\infty
, conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}
, it holds with probability at least
1-\exp(-cL^{1/2})
that

\displaystyle\max_{\vec{z}\in I^{k-1}_{M,L}}\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{sum}}(\vec{z})\geq L+\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}.

To prove this in the
\beta=1
case, the main thing we need is that the contribution to the free energy
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]
from outside
I_{\vec{x}}
is small, which we isolate in the following statement.

Lemma 12.6
.

When
\beta=1
, conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}
, with probability at least
1-\exp(-cML^{1/2})
,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\vec{z}\in[-1,1]^{k-1}\setminus I_{\vec{x}}}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in[-1,1]^{k-1}\setminus I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in[-1,1]^{k-1}\setminus I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in[-1,1]^{k-1}\setminus I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(z_{i-1},s_{i-1};z_{i},s_{i})\leq L-\frac{1}{2}M\log L.

To handle the
\beta=\infty
case, we will need the following statement, which is that the maximizer restricted to
I_{\vec{x}}
is with high probability the same as the global maximize.
We note that it also holds when
\beta=1
.

We define the restricted (in terms of the interval over which the maximization is performed) version of
\pi^{*}(s_{i})
: for
i=1,\ldots,k-1
we let

(12.9)

\displaystyle\pi^{*,\mathrm{res},\vec{x}}(s_{i})=\argmax_{|z|\leq ML^{-1/2}\log L}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,s_{i-1};x_{i}L^{-1/4}+z,s_{i})+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{i}L^{-1/4}+z,s_{i};0,s_{i+1})\right),

for
M
being a large number.

Lemma 12.7
.

With probability
1-\exp(-cML^{1/2}\log L)
conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
, for each
i=1,\ldots,k
,

\displaystyle\pi^{*}(s_{i})=x_{i}L^{-1/4}+\pi^{*,\mathrm{res},\vec{x}}(s_{i}).

We give the proof of Lemma
12.5
before turning to proving Lemmas
12.6
and
12.7
.

Proof of Lemma
12.5
.

In this proof, by ‘conditional probability’, we refer to the conditioning
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}
.

Case of
\beta=\infty
:
We let
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{eq}}
denote the event where
\pi^{*}(s_{i})=x_{i}L^{-1/4}+\pi^{*,\mathrm{res},\vec{x}}(s_{i})
for each
i=1,\ldots,k-1
.
We first note that by
Lemma

12.7
and (
12.1
), for all large enough
M
,
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{eq}}
holds with conditional probability
>1-\exp(-cML^{1/2})
.
Now on
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{eq}}
it follows by definition that
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]=\max_{\vec{z}\in I_{\vec{x}}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(z_{i-1},s_{i-1};z_{i},s_{i})
. Further by coalescence, which holds with conditional probability
>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
by
Proposition

3.4
and (
12.1
), it follows that

\max_{\vec{z}\in I^{k}_{M,L}}\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{sum}}(\vec{z})=\max_{\vec{z}\in I_{\vec{x}}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(z_{i-1},s_{i-1};z_{i},s_{i})+\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\geq L+\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i},

completing the proof.

Case of
\beta=1
: By Lemma
12.1
there exists
\rho>0
such that, with conditional probability at least
1-CL^{-1}
, we have
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]\geq L+\rho L^{-3}
.
By Lemma
12.6
, with conditional probability at least
1-\exp(-cML^{1/2})
,

\displaystyle\exp(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1])-\exp\left(\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\vec{z}\in I_{\vec{x}}}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(z_{i-1},s_{i-1};z_{i},s_{i})\right)\leq L^{-\frac{1}{2}M}\exp(L).

The previous two inequalities, along with
\exp(x)\geq 1+x
, imply that

\displaystyle\exp\left(\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\vec{z}\in I_{\vec{x}}}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(z_{i-1},s_{i-1};z_{i},s_{i})\right)

\displaystyle\geq\exp(L+\rho L^{-3})-L^{-\frac{1}{2}M}\exp(L)

\displaystyle\geq\exp(L)\left[1+\rho L^{-3}-L^{-\frac{1}{2}M}\right]

\displaystyle\geq\exp(L)\left[1+\tfrac{1}{2}\rho L^{-3}\right].

By coalescence (
Proposition

3.4
), with conditional probability at least
1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
,
