\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\vec{z}\in I^{k}_{M,L}}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I^{k}_{M,L}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I^{k}_{M,L}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I^{k}_{M,L}}^{(\beta)}}}\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{sum}}(\vec{z})\geq\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\vec{z}\in I_{\vec{x}}}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(z_{i-1},s_{i-1};z_{i},s_{i})+\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}-kCe^{-cL}\\
>L+\tfrac{1}{4}\rho L^{-3}-kCe^{-cL}+\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i},

Since
kCe^{-cL}\ll\rho L^{-1}
, the proof is complete by relabeling
\rho
.
∎

The main step in proving Lemma
12.6
is to handle the case of
k=2
, which is given in the following statement; then we can obtain the statement for general
k
by making use of coalescence (Proposition
3.4
).

Lemma 12.8
.

Conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}
, it holds with probability at least
1-\exp(-cML^{1/2})
that, for each
i=1,\ldots,k-1
,

\displaystyle\int_{(-x_{i}L^{-1/4}+[-1,1])\setminus I_{M,L}}\exp\bigl(\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{x}}_{1}(z)+\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{bot},i+1,\vec{x}}_{1}(z)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z\leq L^{-M}\exp\left(h_{i}+h_{i+1}\right).

and

\displaystyle\int_{-x_{i}L^{-1/4}+[-1,1]}\exp\bigl(\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{x}}_{1}(z)+\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{bot},i+1,\vec{x}}_{1}(z)\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}z\leq\exp\left(h_{i}+h_{i+1}-\log(2L^{1/2})+M\right).

Proof.

The second inequality follows from (
9.6
) in
Lemma

9.3
and (
12.1
).

For the first inequality, by (
12.1
), it suffices to show that the displayed inequality holds with probability at least
1-\exp(-cML^{1/2})
given only that
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
.
Indeed, conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
, from
Corollary

2.21
, it holds with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cML^{1/2}\log L)
that, for all
z\not\in I_{M,L}
,
z+x_{i}L^{-1/4}\in[-1,1]
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{x}}_{1}(z)

\displaystyle\leq h_{i}-\tfrac{1}{2}M\log L-\tfrac{1}{4}L^{1/2}|z|,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{bot},i+1,\vec{x}}_{1}(z)

\displaystyle\leq h_{i+1}-\tfrac{1}{2}M\log L-\tfrac{1}{4}L^{1/2}|z|.

By plugging these into the integral, the conclusion follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
12.6
.

By coalescence
Proposition

3.4
and (
12.1
),
with probability at least
1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
conditional on
\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{cond}}
, the LHS is at most

\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\vec{z}+L^{-1/4}\vec{x}\in[-1,1]^{k-1}\setminus I_{\vec{x}}}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}+L^{-1/4}\vec{x}\in[-1,1]^{k-1}\setminus I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}+L^{-1/4}\vec{x}\in[-1,1]^{k-1}\setminus I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}+L^{-1/4}\vec{x}\in[-1,1]^{k-1}\setminus I_{\vec{x}}}^{(\beta)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{x}}_{1}(z_{i})+\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{bot},i+1,\vec{x}}_{1}(z_{i})+Ce^{-cL}-\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}h_{i}.

Recall that the first term is the logarithm of an integral over
[-1,1]^{k-1}\setminus I_{\vec{x}}
. We can upper bound the integral by replacing
[-1,1]^{k-1}\setminus I_{\vec{x}}
with the
k-1
dimension product where all but the
i
th
one is
[-1,1]
and the
i
th
one is
[-1,1]\setminus(x_{i}L^{-1/4}+I_{M,L})
, and summing over
i
(i.e., the sum is inside the logarithm).
Using
Lemma

12.8
, we get an upper bound of

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}+Ce^{-cL}-M\log L-(k-2)\log(2L^{1/2})+M+C\log k.

Using that
\vec{h}\in E_{\mathrm{val}}
, by taking
M
large enough the conclusion follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
12.7
.

We need to show that with high probability, conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
, it holds that
\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq ML^{-1/2}\log L
so that
\pi^{*}(s_{i})=x_{i}L^{-1/4}+\pi^{*,\mathrm{res},\vec{x}}(s_{i})
.

Let
J_{i}
denote the interval
\{z:|z+x_{i}L^{-1/4}|\leq 10^{-6}(s_{i}-s_{i-1})^{1/2}\wedge(s_{i+1}-s_{i})^{1/2}\}
.
By
Proposition

3.4
, conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
, with probability
>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
the event
\pi^{*}(s_{i})\neq x_{i}L^{-1/4}+\pi^{*,\mathrm{res},\vec{x}}(s_{i})

implies that

(12.10)

\sup_{z\in J_{i}\setminus I_{M,L}}\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{x}}(z)+\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\mathrm{bot},i+1,\vec{x}}(z)>h_{i}+h_{i+1}-C\exp(-cL),

or

(12.11)

\sup_{z\not\in J_{i}}\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,s_{i-1};x_{i}L^{-1/4}+z,s_{i})+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{i}L^{-1/4}+z,s_{i};0,s_{i+1})\\
\geq\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,s_{i-1};x_{i}L^{-1/4},s_{i})+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{i}L^{-1/4},s_{i};0,s_{i+1}).

We have that (
12.10
) further implies that either

\sup_{z\in J_{i}\setminus I_{M,L}}\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{x}}(z)>h_{i+1}-C\exp(-cL),

or

\sup_{z\in J_{i}\setminus I_{M,L}}\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\mathrm{bot},i+1,\vec{x}}(z)>h_{i}-C\exp(-cL),

happens; and by
Corollary

2.21
, each happens, conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
, with probability
<\exp(-cML^{1/2}\log L)
.

As for (
12.11
), by
Corollary

2.21
, with probability
>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/4})
conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
, we have
that the RHS is
>h_{i}+h_{i+1}-L^{1/4}\log L
.
For the LHS, for any fixed
z\not\in J_{i}
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(0,s_{i-1};x_{i}L^{-1/4}+z,s_{i})+\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(x_{i}L^{-1/4}+z,s_{i};0,s_{i+1})>h_{i}+h_{i+1}-L^{1/4}\log L\right)\\
<C\exp\left(-\tfrac{4}{3}(s_{i+1}-s_{i-1})L^{3/2}-cz^{2}L^{1/2}\right)

by
Theorem

2.13
, and that
\vec{h}\in E_{\mathrm{val}}
.
Then by a union bound over all
z\not\in J_{i}
,
z\in L^{-2}L
, and using the continuity estimate
Proposition

2.16
, we get that the LHS is
\leq h_{i}+h_{i+1}-L^{1/4}\log L
with probability
>1-C\exp\left(-\tfrac{4}{3}(s_{i+1}-s_{i-1})L^{3/2}-cL^{3/2}\right)
.
Then since

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\in h_{i}+[0,e^{-L}]\right)>c\exp\left(-\tfrac{4}{3}(s_{i}-s_{i-1})L^{3/2}-CL^{3/4}\right)

and

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i+1}\in h_{i+1}+[0,e^{-L}]\right)>c\exp\left(-\tfrac{4}{3}(s_{i+1}-s_{i})L^{3/2}-CL^{3/4}\right),

by
Theorem

2.13
, we conclude that (
12.11
) happens with probability
<C\exp(-cL^{3/4})
conditional on
\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

12.3.
The density comparison

We now finish the proof of
Lemma

11.3
.

Recall the setup at the beginning of this section. Also recall the definition of
\pi^{*,\mathrm{res},\vec{x}}(s_{i})
from (
12.9
) as a version of
\pi^{*}(s_{i})
defined for
i=1,\ldots,k-1
but with a restricted interval over which the maximization is performed, and the definition of
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{x}}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{bot},i,\vec{x}}_{1}

from (
12.7
), and
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{sum}}
from (
12.8
).

As before, we let
I_{M,L}=[-ML^{-1/2}\log L,ML^{-1/2}\log L]
, and
w_{\beta=1}=L^{-1/2}
,
w_{\beta=\infty}=L^{-1}
.

Proof of
Lemma

11.3
.

We want to estimate the conditional probabilities on both the LHS and the RHS and show that they are within
1+o(1)
of each other.
Towards this, for either
\iota=0
or
1
(we allow both as the RHS in Lemma
11.3
does not have the
\smash{\beta^{-1}e^{-cL^{1/4}}}
term), we claim that

(12.12)

\mathbb{P}\Bigl(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|<w_{\beta},\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]>(1-\iota\beta^{-1}e^{-cL^{1/4}})L\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}\Bigr)=(1+O(L^{-1}))\\
\times\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i=1,\ldots,k-1}|\pi^{*}(s_{i})-x_{i}L^{-1/4}|<w_{\beta},\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}}\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{sum}}(\vec{z})\geq L+\beta^{-1}\rho L^{-3}+\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\ \Big|\ \vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}\right).

Indeed, by Lemma
12.5
, the LHS is upper bounded by the RHS; while by
Proposition

3.4
,

\mathcal{L}^{\beta}[1]+\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}_{i}\geq\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}}\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{sum}}(\vec{z}),

with probability
>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
, conditional on
\mathcal{L}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h}
.
Then since the LHS is lower bounded by
c\exp(-CL^{1/2})
(
\beta=1
) or
c
(
\beta=\infty
) by
Proposition

9.1
and
\vec{h}\in E_{\mathrm{val}}
, the LHS is lower bounded by the RHS.

We now let
\mathcal{E}_{\pm}
denote the events where for each
i=1,\ldots,k-1
,

\sup_{|z|\leq w_{\beta}}\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{x}}_{1}(z)+\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{bot},i+1,\vec{x}}_{1}(z)>\sup_{|z|\geq w_{\beta},z\in I_{M,L}}\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{x}}_{1}(z)+\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{bot},i+1,\vec{x}}_{1}(z)\mp C\exp(-cL),

and

\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}}\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{sum}}(\vec{z})\geq L+\beta^{-1}\rho L^{-3}+\sum_{i=2}^{k-1}h_{i}\mp C\exp(-cL).

By
Lemma

12.7
and
Proposition

9.1
,
the probability in the RHS of (
12.12
) is in

(12.13)

\left[\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{-}\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h})-\exp(-cML^{1/2}),\;\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{+}\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h})+\exp(-cML^{1/2})\right].

Now we wish to make use of
Proposition

3.5
to replace the processes
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{x}}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{bot},i,\vec{x}}_{1}
by a collection of processes given by independent Brownian bridges, up to an exponentially small
L^{\infty}
error and on an event with probability
1-\exp(-cL)
.

Denote

G=L+\beta^{-1}\rho L^{-3}-\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}

Since
\vec{h}\in E_{\mathrm{val}}
from (
11.5
), we have
G<-(k-1)\beta^{-1}\log(2L^{1/2})+Cr_{\beta}
(recall that
r_{\beta=1}=1
and
r_{\beta=\infty}=L^{-1/2}
).

Reduce to Brownian bridges
.
We denote
\theta=10^{-7}\min_{i=1,\ldots,k}(s_{i}-s_{i-1})L^{1/2}
.
We let
\Theta
denote the collection of all

\vec{b}=(\vec{b}^{\mathrm{bot},\mathrm{L}},\vec{b}^{\mathrm{bot},\mathrm{R}},\vec{b}^{\mathrm{top},\mathrm{L}},\vec{b}^{\mathrm{top},\mathrm{R}})=\bigl((b^{\mathrm{bot},\mathrm{L}}_{i})_{i=2}^{k},(b^{\mathrm{bot},\mathrm{R}}_{i})_{i=2}^{k},(b^{\mathrm{top},\mathrm{L}}_{i})_{i=1}^{k-1},(b^{\mathrm{top},\mathrm{R}}_{i})_{i=1}^{k-1}\bigr)\in\mathbb{R}^{4(k-1)}.

For any
\vec{b}\in\Theta
, we denote by
B^{\dagger,i,\vec{b}}
a rate
2
Brownian bridge from
(-\theta,-2\theta L^{1/2}+b^{\mathrm{\dagger},\mathrm{L}}_{i})
, to
(0,0)
, then to
(\theta,-2\theta L^{1/2}+b^{\mathrm{\dagger},\mathrm{R}}_{i})
,
for
\dagger=\mathrm{top}
,
i=1,\ldots,k-1
, and
\dagger=\mathrm{bot}
,
i=2,\ldots,k
.
All these
B^{\dagger,i}
are independent of each other. One should think of
B^{\dagger,i}
as being approximately
\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\dagger,i,\vec{x}}-h_{i}
, which we will use in a more precise sense via Proposition
3.5
.

We then denote by
\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}}
the event where for each

i=1,\ldots,k-1
,

\sup_{|z|\leq w_{\beta}}B^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{b}}(z)+B^{\mathrm{bot},i+1,\vec{b}}(z)>\sup_{|z|\geq w_{\beta},z\in I_{M,L}}B^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{b}}(z)+B^{\mathrm{bot},i+1,\vec{b}}(z),

and

\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}B^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{b}}(z_{i})+\sum_{i=2}^{k}B^{\mathrm{bot},i,\vec{b}}(z_{i-1})\geq G.

We also denote by
\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b},\pm}
the event where for each

i=1,\ldots,k-1
,

\sup_{|z|\leq w_{\beta}}B^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{b}}(z)+B^{\mathrm{bot},i+1,\vec{b}}(z)>\sup_{|z|\geq w_{\beta},z\in I_{M,L}}B^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{b}}(z)+B^{\mathrm{bot},i+1,\vec{b}}(z)\mp C\exp(-cL),

and

\mathop{\mathchoice{\operatorname*{max^{(\beta)\kern-11.28444pt}}_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}\kern 0.0pt}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}{\emaxop_{\vec{z}\in I_{M,L}^{k-1}}^{(\beta)}}}\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}B^{\mathrm{top},i,\vec{b}}(z_{i})+\sum_{i=2}^{k}B^{\mathrm{bot},i,\vec{b}}(z_{i-1})\geq G\mp C\exp(-cL).

It is straightforward to check that

(12.14)

|\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}})-\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b},\pm})|<C\exp(-cL).

For each
a>0
we let
\Theta_{a}
denote the collection of all
\vec{b}\in\Theta
, where each coordinate is in
[-a,a]
.
We write
D=\log L
.

Since
\vec{x}\in\mathcal{K}
which is a compact set, applying
Proposition

3.5
, and using
Lemma

2.20
to bound each
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{top},i}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{h}^{\mathrm{bot},i}_{1}
at
\pm\theta
, we have that

(12.15)

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{-}\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h})\geq(1-C\exp(-cD^{2}))\min_{\vec{b}\in\Theta_{DL^{1/4}}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b},-})-C\exp(-cL),

and

(12.16)

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{+}\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h})\leq\max_{\vec{b}\in\Theta_{DL^{1/4}}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b},+})+\sum_{i=2}^{\lfloor L^{1/4}\rfloor}C\exp(-ci^{2}D^{2})\max_{\vec{b}\in\Theta_{iDL^{1/4}}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b},+})\\
+C\exp(-cL)+C\exp(-cD^{2}L^{1/2}).

By (
12.14
), we can replace each
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b},\pm})
by
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}})
.
We also note that these bounds are independent of
\vec{x}
.

We next state a comparison lemma for different
\vec{b}
.

Lemma 12.9
.

For any large enough
K>0
, and
\vec{b},\vec{g}\in\Theta
such that
\|\vec{b}-\vec{g}\|_{\infty}<KL^{1/4}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}})=(1+O(K^{2}L^{-1/4}\log L))\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{g}})+O(\exp(-cK^{2}M^{-1}L^{1/2}\log L)).

Now for each
i=1,\ldots,\lfloor L^{1/4}\rfloor
, and any
\vec{b}\in\Theta_{iDL^{1/4}}
,
\vec{g}\in\Theta_{DL^{1/4}}
, we can find a sequence of vectors in
\Theta
,
\vec{b}=\vec{b}^{(0)},\vec{b}^{(1)},\ldots,\vec{b}^{(i)}=\vec{g}
, with
\|\vec{b}^{(j-1)}-\vec{b}^{(j)}\|_{\infty}\leq DL^{1/4}
for each
j=1,\ldots,i
.
Then (
12.9
) implies that for each
j

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}^{(j-1)}})<(1+CD^{2}L^{-1/4}\log L)\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}^{(j)}})+C\exp(-cD^{2}M^{-1}L^{1/2}\log L),

and, since
1+x\leq\exp(x)
,

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}})<\exp(CiD^{2}L^{-1/4}\log L)\left(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{g}})+C\exp(-cD^{2}M^{-1}L^{1/2}\log L)\right).

In other words, we have (for each
i=1,\ldots,\lfloor L^{1/4}\rfloor
)

\max_{\vec{b}\in\Theta_{iDL^{1/4}}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}})<\exp(CiD^{2}L^{-1/4}\log L)\left(\min_{\vec{b}\in\Theta_{DL^{1/4}}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}})+C\exp(-cD^{2}M^{-1}L^{1/2}\log L)\right).

Therefore, by plugging this into (
12.16
) (with each
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b},+})
replaced by
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}})
using (
12.14
)) we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{+}\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h})<(1+C\exp(-cD^{2}))\max_{\vec{b}\in\Theta_{DL^{1/4}}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}})+C\exp(-cD^{2}M^{-1}L^{1/2}\log L).

Also, (
12.15
) (with each
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b},-})
replaced by
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}})
using (
12.14
)) and
Lemma

12.9
imply that

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{-}\mid\vec{\mathcal{L}}^{\beta,\vec{x}}\approx\vec{h})>(1-C\exp(-cD^{2}))\max_{\vec{b}\in\Theta_{DL^{1/4}}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}})-C\exp(-cD^{2}M^{-1}L^{1/2}\log L).

Since that (as we have seen above) (
12.12
) is lower bounded by
c\exp(-CL^{1/2})
(
\beta=1
) or
c
(
\beta=\infty
) by
Proposition

9.1
, and the RHS of (
12.12
) is in the interval (
12.13
), we conclude that (
12.12
) equals
(1+o(1))\max_{\vec{b}\in\Theta_{DL^{1/4}}}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}})
, which is independent of
\vec{x}
.
∎

It remains to prove the comparison lemma on Brownian bridges.

Proof of
Lemma

12.9
.

We note that the events
\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\vec{g}}
are measurable with respect to the processes
B^{\dagger,i,\vec{b}}
in the interval
I_{M,L}
.
Therefore, conditional on that each
B^{\dagger,i,\vec{b}}
and
B^{\dagger,i,\vec{g}}
are the same at
\pm ML^{-1/2}\log L
, the conditional probabilities of
\mathcal{E}_{\vec{b}}
and
\mathcal{E}_{\vec{g}}
would be the same.

We let
\mathrm{BdyCtrl}
be the event where for each
\dagger=\mathrm{top}
,
i=1,\ldots,k-1
, and
\dagger=\mathrm{bot}
,
i=2,\ldots,k
, we have

|B^{\dagger,i,\vec{b}}(ML^{-1/2}\log L)-b^{\dagger,\mathrm{R}}_{i}\theta^{-1}ML^{-1/2}\log L+2M\log L|<K\log L,

|B^{\dagger,i,\vec{b}}(-ML^{-1/2}\log L)-b^{\dagger,\mathrm{L}}_{i}\theta^{-1}ML^{-1/2}\log L+2M\log L|<K\log L,

|B^{\dagger,i,\vec{g}}(ML^{-1/2}\log L)-b^{\dagger,\mathrm{R}}_{i}\theta^{-1}ML^{-1/2}\log L+2M\log L|<K\log L,

|B^{\dagger,i,\vec{g}}(-ML^{-1/2}\log L)-b^{\dagger,\mathrm{L}}_{i}\theta^{-1}ML^{-1/2}\log L+2M\log L|<K\log L.

It follows from Gaussian tail bounds (
Lemma

2.22
) (and noting that
\|\vec{b}-\vec{g}\|\leq KL^{1/4}
) that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{BdyCtrl}^{c}\right)\leq\exp\left(-cK^{2}M^{-1}L^{1/2}\log L\right).

It then suffices to control the ratio of the probability densities of
B^{\dagger,i,\vec{b}}(\pm ML^{-1/2}\log L)
and
B^{\dagger,i,\vec{g}}(\pm ML^{-1/2}\log L)
, in the interval
-ML^{1/2}\log L+[-K\log L,K\log L]
.
For this, note that
B^{\dagger,i,\vec{b}}(\pm ML^{-1/2}\log L)
and
B^{\dagger,i,\vec{g}}(\pm ML^{-1/2}\log L)
are Gaussian random variables with the same variance of order
ML^{-1/2}\log L
, and mean differ by
\leq K\theta^{-1}ML^{-1/4}\log L
.
Therefore the density ratio is

\exp(O((K\log L)(K\theta^{-1}ML^{-1/4}\log L)(ML^{-1/2}\log L)^{-1}))=1+O(K^{2}L^{-1/4}\log L).

Therefore the conclusion follows.
∎

Appendix A
Weak convergence lemma

Proof of Lemma
11.2
.

Fix
M>0
.
We then have that

(A.1)

\max_{\|\vec{x}\|_{\infty},\|\vec{y}\|_{\infty}\leq M,\|\vec{x}-\vec{y}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon}\left|\frac{f(\vec{x})}{f(\vec{y})}-1\right|\to 1.

This follows from the continuity and strict positivity of
f
combined with the compactness of
[-M,M]^{d}
.
Now we take any
\delta>0
, and
\vec{x},\vec{y}\in[-M+\delta,M-\delta]^{d}
.
For any
\varepsilon
such that
\delta\varepsilon^{-1}\in\mathbb{N}
, by splitting
\vec{x}+[-\delta,\delta)^{d}
into
(\delta\varepsilon^{-1})^{d}
many translations of
[-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)^{d}
, applying (
11.1
) across them, and using (
A.1
), we have

\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\vec{X}_{\varepsilon}\in\vec{x}+[-\delta,\delta)^{d}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\vec{X}_{\varepsilon}\in\vec{y}+[-\delta,\delta)^{d}\right)}\to\frac{\int_{\vec{x}+[-\delta,\delta)^{d}}f(\vec{z})\,\mathrm{d}\vec{z}}{\int_{\vec{y}+[-\delta,\delta)^{d}}f(\vec{z})\,\mathrm{d}\vec{z}},

as
\epsilon\to 0
.
Then by the continuity of
f
, and that
\vec{X}_{\epsilon}\to\vec{X}
in distribution, we conclude that

\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\vec{X}\in\vec{x}+[-\delta,\delta)^{d}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\vec{X}\in\vec{y}+[-\delta,\delta)^{d}\right)}=\frac{\int_{\vec{x}+[-\delta,\delta)^{d}}f(\vec{z})\,\mathrm{d}\vec{z}}{\int_{\vec{y}+[-\delta,\delta)^{d}}f(\vec{z})\,\mathrm{d}\vec{z}}.

We note that by sending
M\to\infty
, this holds for arbitrary
\vec{x},\vec{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then by the integrability of
f
, we get

\mathbb{P}\left(\vec{X}\in\vec{x}+[-\delta,\delta)^{d}\right)=\frac{\int_{\vec{x}+[-\delta,\delta)^{d}}f(\vec{z})\,\mathrm{d}\vec{z}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(\vec{z})\,\mathrm{d}\vec{z}}.

Then the conclusion follows by sending
\delta\to 0
.
∎

Appendix B
Tent Brownian comparison and estimates

In this appendix, we provide the proofs of
Lemma

2.18
,
Lemma

2.19
, and
Lemma

2.20
.

Proof of
Lemma

2.18
.

We wish to prove
Lemma

2.18
from
Theorem

2.17
, by applying the tail comparison of
Theorem

4.1
. However, the proof of
Theorem

4.1
we give uses
Lemma

2.18
, and we next explain how circular arguments are avoided.

First, recall the notation
L_{M}^{1/2}=(L-M)^{1/2}
from the proof of
Theorem

4.1
. That proof used
Lemma

2.18
in the special case of

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{x\in\{-L_{M}^{1/2},L_{M}^{1/2}\}}\Bigl|\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(x)-L+2L^{1/2}|x|\Bigr|>ML^{1/4}\ \Big|\ \hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right)<\exp(-cM^{2}),

for
M=\log L
, i.e., the process is considered only at the two points
\pm L_{M}^{1/2}
and not on the entire interval
[-L^{1/2},L^{1/2}]
; this was done in the proof of
Theorem

4.1
right before (
4.8
). This application of
Lemma

2.18
with this value of
M
is the only one in the proof of Theorem
4.1
. We will show how to prove the above display, and then we will be allowed to use Theorem
4.1
to prove
Lemma

2.18
.

Call the event in the previous display
A_{M,L}
. Take a large
C_{1}>0
, then the previous display is bounded by

(B.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(A_{M,L},\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)<L+C_{1}L^{1/4}\mid\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+C_{1}L^{1/4}\mid\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right).

The second term is upper bounded, using a trivial upper bound and then Theorem
2.13
, by

(B.2)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+C_{1}L^{1/4}\right)}{\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right)}\leq\frac{\exp\left(-\frac{4}{3}(L+C_{1}L^{1/4})^{3/2}+CL^{3/4}\right)}{\exp\left(-\frac{4}{3}L^{3/2}-CL^{3/4}\right)}\leq\exp\left(-cL^{3/4}\right),

as
C_{1}
is large.
The first term of (
B.1
) is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(A_{M,L}\ \Big|\ \hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)\in(L,L+C_{1}L^{1/4})\right)\leq\sup_{L^{\prime}\in(L,L+C_{1}L^{1/4})}\mathbb{P}\left(A_{M,L}\ \Big|\ \hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)\in(L^{\prime},L^{\prime}+\mathrm{d}L^{\prime})\right).

Now applying the
Theorem

2.17
where we condition on the value of
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)
yields that the final term is upper bounded by
\exp(-cM^{2})
when
0<M<cL^{3/4}
. Thus the bound on (
B.1
) is
\exp(-cM^{2})+\exp(-cL^{3/4})\leq\exp(-cM^{2})
when
M<L^{3/8}
, as is certainly the case for
M=\log L
.

This establishes the needed bound on (
B.1
) and so we may now make use of Theorem
4.1
, as noted above. We now perform the same analysis as above but with

\displaystyle\tilde{A}_{M,L}=\left\{\sup_{|x|\leq L^{1/2}}\Bigl|\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(x)-L+2L^{1/2}|x|\Bigr|>ML^{1/4}\right\}

replacing
A_{M,L}
and with
M^{2}L^{-1/2}
replacing
C_{1}L^{1/4}
; the only difference is that the bound in (
B.2
) now follows from Theorem
4.1
instead of Theorem
2.13
(and is now
\exp(-cM^{2})
instead of
\exp(-cL^{3/4})
). The remaining analysis is the same and yields an overall bound on the LHS of (
B.1
) of
\exp(-cM^{2})
for
0<M<cL^{3/4}
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

2.19
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{0}
denote the event where
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)\in(L,L+\,\mathrm{d}L)
.
Denote by
\mathcal{E}_{1}
the event

\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,2}(x)<-x^{2}+0.1L,\quad\forall x\in[-\tfrac{1}{2}L^{1/2},\tfrac{1}{2}L^{1/2}],

and by
\mathcal{E}_{2}
the event where

\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(-L^{1/2}/2),\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(L^{1/2}/2)>-0.1L.

By
Lemma

2.12
and
Lemma

3.2
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\mid\mathcal{E}_{0})>\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(x)<-x^{2}+0.1L,\;\forall x\in[-\tfrac{1}{2}L^{1/2},\tfrac{1}{2}L^{1/2}]\right)>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2}).

By
Theorem

2.17
we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2}\mid\mathcal{E}_{0})>1-C\exp(-cL^{3/2})
.

We now assume the event
\mathcal{E}_{0}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}
, and consider the Radon-Nikodym derivative between the two sets of processes.
Let
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{ext}}([-t^{2/3}L^{1/2}/2,t^{2/3}L^{1/2}/2])
be the sigma-algebra generated by
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}
on
(-\infty,-L^{1/2}/2]\cup\{0\}\cup[L^{1/2}/2,\infty)
and
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,2}
.
Then according to the Gibbs property (
Lemma

2.9
),
it suffices to bound

(B.3)

Z^{-1}W(B,\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2}),

where

\bullet

\hat{B}:[-L^{1/2}/2,L^{1/2}/2]\to\mathbb{R}
is a rate
2
Brownian bridge, conditional on
\hat{B}(-L^{1/2}/2)=\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(-L^{1/2}/2)
,
\hat{B}(0)=L
,
\hat{B}(L^{1/2}/2)=\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(L^{1/2}/2)
; and
B:[-t^{2/3}L^{1/2}/2,t^{2/3}L^{1/2}/2]\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies that
\hat{B}(x)=t^{-1/3}B(t^{2/3}x)
;

\bullet

W(B,\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2})<1
is the weight defined through (
2.6
), in the interval
[-t^{2/3}L^{1/2}/2,t^{2/3}L^{1/2}/2]
;

\bullet

Z=\mathbb{E}[W(B,\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2})\mid\mathcal{F}]
is a renormalization constant.

Assuming
\mathcal{E}_{0}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\hat{B}(x)\geq 0.2L,\forall x\in[-L^{1/2}/2,L^{1/2}/2]\mid\mathcal{F})>1-C\exp(-cL^{2})
; and whenever
\inf_{x\in[-L^{1/2}/2,L^{1/2}/2]}\hat{B}(x)\geq 0.2L
and also under
\mathcal{E}_{1}
, we have

\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2}(t^{2/3}x)-B(t^{2/3}x)>t^{1/3}\cdot 0.1L,\quad\forall x\in[-L^{1/2}/2,L^{1/2}/2].

Therefore, from (
2.6
) we have

W(B,\mathfrak{h}^{\beta}_{t,2})>1-2t^{2/3}L^{1/2}\exp(-t^{1/3}\cdot 0.1L)>1-C\exp(-ct^{1/3}L).

Thus we have
Z>1-C\exp(-ct^{1/3}L)
, and (
B.3
) is
1+O(\exp(-ct^{1/3}L))
under
\mathcal{E}_{0}\cap\mathcal{E}_{1}\cap\mathcal{E}_{2}
.
This combined with the estimates on
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\mid\mathcal{E}_{0})
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{2}\mid\mathcal{E}_{0})
leads to the conclusion.
∎

We next prove
Lemma

2.20
.

Proof of
Lemma

2.20
.

For the first bound, by
Theorem

2.17
we have that

\mathbb{P}\left(|\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(\pm L^{1/2}/2)|>ML^{1/4}/5\ \Big|\ \hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)\in(L,L+\,\mathrm{d}L)\right)<\exp(-cM^{2}).

Then the bound follows from
Lemma

2.19
and standard Brownian bridge estimates.

For the second bound, we use a strategy similar to the proof of
Lemma

2.18
. Namely, the LHS is bounded by

\displaystyle\sup_{L^{\prime}\in(L,L+M\sigma_{I}/2)}\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{x\in I}\left|\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(x)-(L-2L^{1/2}|x|)\right|>M\sigma_{I}\ \Big|\ \hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)\in(L^{\prime},L^{\prime}+\,\mathrm{d}L^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L+M\sigma_{I}/2\ \Big|\ \hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>L\right).

Then by the first bound and
Theorem

4.1
, the second bound follows.
∎

Appendix C
Tail and tent estimates for small time

In this appendix we explain how to adapt the proofs in
[
GH22
]
to get
Theorem

2.13
and
Theorem

2.17
, using inputs from
[
DG23
]
. Theorems
2.13
and
2.17
are proven in
[
GH22
]
for
t>t_{0}
for any
t_{0}
fixed, so here we prove it for
0<t<t_{0}
for a particular
t_{0}
coming from the new inputs from
[
DG23
]
.

Written with our scaling,
[
DG23
, Theorem 1.4]
asserts that, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exist
t_{0}
,
c
, and
s_{0}
, all depending on
\varepsilon
, such that, for
0<t<t_{0}
and
M>M_{0}t^{-1/12}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>M+t^{-1/3}\log t^{-1}\right)\leq\exp\left(-c\frac{M^{2}t^{1/6}}{\sqrt{1+Mt^{1/3-\varepsilon/2}}}\right).

If we additionally assume that
M>M_{0}t^{-1/3-\varepsilon}
and
t<1
, so that
M^{1/2}\geq t^{-1/6-\varepsilon/2}\geq t^{-\varepsilon/2}
, the previous display implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>2M\right)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>M+t^{-1/3}\log t^{-1}\right)\leq\exp\left(-cM^{3/2}t^{\varepsilon/2}\right)\leq\exp(-cM).

For control on the lower tail, as recorded in
Theorem

2.15
,
[
DG23
, Theorem 1.7]
and a similar calculation shows that, if
M>t^{-1/6}
,

(C.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)<-M\right)\leq\exp(-cM^{2}t^{1/6})\leq\exp(-cM);

and one can also instead assume
M>t^{-1/12-\varepsilon}
and obtain a weaker bound of the form
\exp(-cM^{\alpha})
for some
\alpha=\alpha(\varepsilon)
.

To apply the arguments of
[
GH22
]
, we also need an a priori lower bound on
\mathbb{P}(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>M)
. For this it is easy to check that the proof of
[
GH22
, Lemma 5.4]
applies verbatim if we assume
M>\smash{t^{-1/6}}
(or
M>\smash{t^{-1/12-\varepsilon}}
, as above) and use (
C.1
) in place of lower tail tightness (over
t\geq t_{0}
) of
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)
. This will then yield, for
t>0
and
M>t^{-1/6}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t,1}(0)>M\right)\geq\exp(-5M^{3/2}).

With these estimates in place, we satisfy Assumptions (i)–(iv) from
[
GH22
, Section 2.2]
(more precisely, the above verifies Assumption (iv) on tail bounds, as the other assumptions are qualitative and hold for any
t>0
). In particular,
[
GH22
, Theorems 4.1, 5.1, and 5.3]
will apply as they hold only assuming these assumptions, modulo controlling the partition function arising from the Gibbs property of
\hat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\beta}_{t}
, and we explain this point next.

In
[
GH22
]
, the estimates lower bounding the partition function are captured in Lemma 3.11, Corollary 3.12, Proposition 3.14, and Corollary 3.15. The latter two are already stated for
t>0
. Lemma 3.11 says that the partition function
Z_{t}
of a single curve with respect to a lower boundary curve
p
on an interval
[z_{1},z_{2}]
is lower bounded by
\exp(-2t^{2/3}e^{-t^{1/6}})\int_{z_{1}}^{z_{2}}\exp(-t^{1/3}g(u))\,\mathrm{d}u)\cdot\mathbb{P}(B(u)>p(u)+g(u)+t^{-1/6},\forall u\in[z_{1},z_{2}])
for any non-negative function
g
, and a quick inspection of the proof shows that a minor modification yields a lower bound of
\exp(-2t^{2/3})\int_{z_{1}}^{z_{2}}\exp(-t^{1/3}g(u))\,\mathrm{d}u)\cdot\mathbb{P}(B(u)>p(u)+g(u),\forall u\in[z_{1},z_{2}])
, which is better for small
t
. Carrying this change to Corollary 3.12 and then making use of these estimates in the proofs of
[
GH22
, Theorems 4.1, and 5.1]
will then yield Theorems
2.13
and
2.17
.
